When the Chain Jumps Oft I have had a number of complaints that buyers of low-priced new machines have been troubled with a jumping chain—an unpleasant event at any time. In a road experience of , twenty todd years I have never once' dropped the tohnin of a cycle of well-known make, but a dheap machine may easily let a man down in this way within a thousand miles. Always the trolible is due to chain-stretch to irregular sprockets to a back wheel hopelessly out of truth. The latter is easily detected when one looks along the machine from behind. Chain- stretch, however, is not so easily spotted, most riders having entirely opposed views as to how tight the chain should be. There should only be a slight sagging of the chain—just enough to ensure unhampered running—when pressed with the finger. When the chain jumps off after the first few miles, of course the trouble is due to the spindle nuts and adjusters not having been properly tightened when the machine left the shop. This can be remedied in a moment. The rider of a reliable mount will never have trouble with the chain jumping— one of many reasons why it pays to have a good bicycle.

Loss of Compression. It is often found that .after a car has been running for a couple of seasons or so there is a considerable loss of power. If steps are taken to overcome this defect immediately it becomes noticeable a permanent cure can be effected without much trouble or expense. If, on the other hand, the matter is neglected, it will speedily become worse. Very often this loss of power is owing to faulty compression. The most common cause of this defect is due to leakage past the valves. To remedy, the valves must be carefully ground until both the valve seating and the valve face are perfectly smooth. Sometimes loss of compression is due to a leakage between the piston and the cylinder wall. Generally; this is the fault of the piston rings; either they are worn or else badly- fitting. If worn, they must, of course, be re, placed. If they are badly-fitting, it is very easy to remedy. The ring must fit exactly into its proper groove. There must be no slackness; but it must, at the same time, be able to move freely. The ring must touch the cylinder walls evenly all round. In a very old engine loss of compression may be owing to the fact that the cylinders have worn oval, the pistons as well. The only cures for this is a somewhat expensive one—the cyl;nders must be ground true and new pistons provided.

Joy Riding. An active, but grossly unfair crusade is being waged at the present moment against what is termed pleasure motoring. Some of our more sensational and utterly unreliable panic- mongers are declaiming furiously against the sin o'f motoring, and if they are believed— which they are not—we are going to lose the war unless it is instantly put down. We have become so accustomed to these silly and insane statements that they do not really do a great deal of harm. The man in the street is a sensible fellow, on the whole, and he knows pretty correctly what to believe and what not to believe. But it is possible that these remarks may be taken seriously. The statement must therefore be refuted before any further harm is done to an industry which has played a noble part in the war, and of whom so many members, mostly the humble members, have been hit so hard Pleasure motoring is practi- cally entirely a thing of the past. Motor cars are still used, .but to nothing like the same extent as they were before the war, and of those which are used 99 per cent. are used, not for pleasure, but for• philanthropic work or business. If motoring is done away with, thousands of garages—which are now having a difficulty to carry on—will have to shut up. Wounded soldiers will no longer have the bene- fit of getting into the country air for spins, and the thousand and one ways in which motor cars are helping will cease. If only one could prohibit the croakers and those who love to distort the truth, how excellent it would be!

CYCLING AHD MOTORING.

The Campaign Against " Joy- Ride " Extravagance.

OXFORDSHIRE FOR CYCLING.

(Queries and correspondence should be addressed to "Spokes," c/o The Editor. Replies cannot be sent by post, and all queries will be answered in our columns.)

Oxfordshire possesses a variety of charms, apart from its county town, and university, a visit to which is filled with so many delights. But the county itself abounds in thickly- wooded lanes, with here and there expansive commons affording a charming change. Here is a good general see-the-country route:—

Oxford to Betley, 11; to Eynsham, 44; to Witney, 54; to Burford, 84; to Chipping Norton, 11; to Banbury, 13; to Aynho, 64 ; to Bicester, 84; to Waddesdon, 11; to Aylesbury, 51; to Dinton, 4; to Thame, 54; to Shilling- ford, 124; to Nettlebed, 74; to Henley, 44; to Reading, 84; to Pangbourne, 6; to Steventon,

151; to Abingdon, 4; to Oxford, ; total 151 i

Eynsham Church is ancient, and Witney is famous for the manufacture of blankets, whose name is now world-spread. Chipping Nforton, the highest town in Oxfordshire, is a quaint old market town, and Banbury we all know for its cakes, its cheese, and its cross, to which we have all at some time ridden "a -cock- horse." At Bicester there are some fragments of a Saxon church; as well as the walls of a Norman church. There is an excellent run from Bieester to Aylesbury, an old market town, and now the centre of the duck-breeding industry. Nettlebed, a village perched on the top of the Chilterns, is particularly charming, and is on the old coaching road from London to Oxford. It is surrounded on all sides by open commons and superb beech woods, while the views on all sides are very line. The run down into Henley is one of the best I know, the roads being excellent and the descents in the extreme. Perhaps the most charming scenery is encountered on the road between Reading and Steventon via Pangbourne and Goring. The roads run alongside the River Thames nearly the whole of the way, and on a warm summer .day the picture of gliding boats and dainty maidens, of wimpling river set in a border of emerald foliage and rich blue sky is all one that will remain in the memory through many a grey day of after years.

Club Fixtures? Yes, With the speedy approach of the fine weather —according to the calendar, I mean—wheelmen are asking one another, with true British optimism, t` Ought we to fix a programme-, carnival, 10'miles' speed dash, time trial, long distance, and the rest—or is sport unpatriotic, d'you think?" For my part I say emphatically "Yes; fix your programme." How will it help the boys at the Front if, instead of being out on the road, putting your strength ad skill against the other fellow's, you sit at home and mope? I do not say that organised road trials or big meetings are wise. Neither do I think that championship events ought to be settled. So many of our crack-riders, so many of the local "best men," are doing their little bit over the water that it would be grossly unfair to them to award championship honours in any event in which they are unable to compete. But the harmless club fixture which does not even attract the attention of the outside public, let alone divert the public's pocket money to "wrongful channels," should be arranged as usual. The sport of cycling is suffering—no one can deny it—from the war, and some such

VICTIM OF THE DARK.

Cab Driver Killed By Taxi Near Warrington Bridge.

The darkened streets of Warrington have pro- duced their first casualty in the death of a cab driver, who was knocked down by a taxi cab. The sad fact prompted Deputy Coroner F. A. Jones, at the Police Station on Tuesday, to warn the public to exercise greater care, whether they were driving or walking, - than they did in former times.

The victim was George West (55), 8, Sparling- street, Latchford, and the taxi driver was James Ernest Endersby, of Victoria-square, Stockton Heath, who was exonerated from all blanat by the jury. Endersby, who was represented by Mr. Percy Jordan, solicitor, of Manchester, said he was proceeding at about five miles an hour over Warrington Bridge, and just as he got on the bend to turn into Wilderspool-road, he saw another taxi coming along from Knutsford-road. Witness sounded his hem and crossed over in front of him. West was only a few inches in front of the car when he noticed him, and he immediately applied the brakes, pulling up within the car's length. He had two side lamps lighted, but they only cast a reflection of about four yards, and this fact and the darkness of the road, made it impossible for him to see far ahead.

He certainly sounded his horn. Tom Woods, a baker, of 5, Pear Tree-terrace, Who went to the aid of West, said, the taxi was more in the middle of the road than on its proper side. Verdict: "Accidental death.'

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 11, 1916. JOTTINGS BY AN OLD ISUCCESS IN THE POULTRY JOURNALIST. 1 YARD.

Artificial milk we have long heard of, though few of us have tasted it; the artificial egg has now been produced, by "an Austrian scientist." At least, so we read. Presumably the market for eggs for hatching will not be effected. These eggs dissolve in water, and can be used in the preparation of pastry. But when it comes to provisioning the incubator we shall continue to rely on the natural product

The Poultry Yard in March. The busiest time in the poultry yard ljas now arrived (says E. T. Brown in "Farm and Home.") Days off or holidays are impossible during the next few weeks, for the poultry- keeper who is anxious to obtain the most satis- factory results—and where is there one who isn't?—must work from early morning till late at night. Hatching is in full swing; there are many broods of chickens about that require incessant care and attention; there are duck- lings, goslings, and turkey chickens to think L.bout; fattening for the early spring markets is commencing in real earnest; and those who cater for the eggs-for-hatching or day-old chicken trades should realise that this month and the next are the most important from their, point of _view of the whole twelve, and that• not' only is the demand greatest at this period, but prices are at their height. It will thus be seen how much there is to do in the poultry yard, and what a great bearing the work at this time has upon the whole year's results.

Already the days are beginning, to lengthen and there is a feeling of spring in the air. Those who have the land available should re- member what an excellent plan it is to give their fowls as much freedom as possible. After perhaps being confined all the winter the bene- ficial effects of liberty will show themselves in an increased egg supply and added vitality. And then. there is the greatly reduced cost of feeding.

Egg Testing. This is best done by co.operative societies, as recommended by the authorities. Facility in .testing can only be acquired ,by Practice, and for this reason it is desirable that the operator should devote the greater part of his time to the work. Testing is therefore best undertaken when there are large. quantities of eggs to be dealt with; as, for example, at, a Co-operative Egg Depot and in the local. market. The test :might take place where- the eggs are purchased from, the producers, :who could then be 'paid on the basis of quality, and not on the present system of all round prices.• No greater stimulus could be ,given to the production of first-class eggs. Testing and grading are already leading features in the improvements in handling in- troduced by co-operative societies, and are also being adopted by better class local traders, and accommodation should be' provided for the purpose of all local markets. If this were done retailers could then purchase with confidence from local collectors, who, by giving enhanced prices to reliable producers, would in their

turn compel other poultry keepers to aim at the same standard.

Big Eggs.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
The Egg Supply.

A writer quoted in last week's poultry column sounds an alarm respecting this; he declares that' eggs are becoming smaller by degrees and beautifully (?) less. I know one .who is wont to urge that, whatever the size of ,the egg, it represents a day's work—which is about as consoling a reflection as that of an old man whose acquaintance I made wellnigh half a century ago; when anyone remarked in his hearing that flour had advanced in price, he would say "No matter, there's always a penny cake for a penny." When I was in West York- shire, where eggs were ever cheaper than in Lancashire, dealers used to collect large quan- tities during the week, and on the Saturday proceed by workmen's train to the nearest big town in the sister county, and so secure extra profits. " Austria."

The well-known hymn tune so named is not at present in particular favour among us, and, although one may be liable to misapprehension in making such statements, I quite sympathise with "Vigilant's" note upon it in last week's ." Gossip." I have a neighbour who, while he really "can't play for nuts," fancies himself as a pianist, and, despite all remonstrances and warnings that his. windows .are in ,danger, per- sists in grinding out the Austrian Hymn' (which I 'understand to be the air of the German National Anthem), and occasionally even the "Watch by the Rhine." .Trifling as it may seem to some, I cannot understand the fellow's state of 'mind. . - *2 The Poetaster.

Such a person irritates me ever so much more than the harmless and mild mannered indi- vidual who, without any ear for music or talent for rhyming, is everlastingly scribbling what he calls poetry and inflicting it on all and sundry about him. In one district in which I lived some years there were so many of these terrible bores that they formed themselves int 4: a society, with the inexcusably pretentious title "The Valley Poets." There was, however, a certain retributive justice about their pro- ceedings which afforded long-suffering new; paper editors grim satisfaction. It was the members' habit to meet periodically at a given place, and inflict on each other the " pomes " which they had written in the meantine Of course, that is as it should be; one van have nothing but commendation for so excellent an institution—with its inevitable Nemesis.

Long Service. The recognition of Mr. John Willett's 50 years' service at Crosfield's set me thinking of an old compositor who has just died after well- nigh 60 years on the staff of a paper on which I received my training as a youth. When he completed the half-century he was presented with a silver tea service. I was invited to the dinner at which the interesting ceremony took place, and, being honoured with a place on the toast list, was so determined to get my "spoke" in that I all but missed the last train for home. Fancy a man hand-setting and distributing type for one and the same weekly publication for close upon three score years!

Overstrain.

In last week's Cycling Notes there is a timely caution to riders against attempting too much on one day. I had two friends who took to this form of exercise when middle-aged, and were betrayed into the fatal error of overdoing it. They boasted to me of habitually covering the greater part of 100 miles on a fine Sunday; thus,. although between 40 and 50 miles from the coast, they would often take a run to the seaside for the day. That went on for a few years; then one of the pair, . under medical advice, gave up his business and went to live near Bournemouth, where the other week he succumbed. to an affection of the heart. The other, who was master of a public institution, resigned on account of the like symptoms and retired to Blackpool, where the body he so thoughtlessly abused now lies buried. Both were once very robust men, and I am convinced they overstrained their hearts through long- distance cycling, and so shortened their lives.

Ventriloquism. The appearance of a ventriloquist at the Royal Court matinee last week recalled to my mind an interview I once had with a professional in that line, who had many good stories of adven- tures on the road. One was to the effect that the vicar of a parish in which he had halted for a little while called upon him with reference to daily performances at a three days' bazaar. The ventriloquist asked if he would have one, three, or all half dozen dolls, as, of course, the fee varied accordingly. " Oh-h !" exclaimed the reveirend gentleman, with evident surprise, "do you make a charge?" Lent. •

The Rector of Warrington bemoans the period now' entered upon as a particularly sad Lent— and so it is, indeed. I preSume that for the time being many even of those who can afford luxuries will abstain entirely from flesh meat. As one strongly expressed it in the long by- gones

"The cut-throat butchers, wanting throats to cut, • At Lent's approach their bloody shambles shut."

Not that those who observe the llnten fast by changing to fish diet are likely to be better in pocket for it.

The "Good Old Times."

Of course, fish has always been in great demand at this season. Glancing back over the centuries, I note that the food placed before the Earl and Countess Percy about the year 1430 in Lent was bread, " two pieces of salt fish, six baconed (smoked) herrings, four white herrings, and a dish of sprats." In 31, Edward 111., the quantity of fish supplied to the Royal household was 9,000 red herrings, 3,600,whit0 herrings, two barrels of sturgeon, 1,300 stock fish, 89 congers, 320 mulwells. One dish seems to have fallen into disuse. Herring pies were a much appreciated article of diet. What was the price in those days? The figure paid for the above works out at an average of nine red herrings for eightpence, five white herrings for fourpence, stock fish at 50 for Is. Id., congers about 2d. each, and mulwells (probably mullet) 16 for 13s. 4d.

The Elizabethan Period.

The foregoing particulars are copied from old acCounts, which record aLso the price of fish in the spacious days of great Elizabeth. I add these for the sake of comparison. Here are the prices :—A salt salmon, a fresh salmon, a salt fish, and two salt eels, 25.; red herring and a hundred sprats, is.; a peck of cockles, 4d.; a pike and a bream, 3s. Bd.; two dozen dace and a perch, 5d.; a peck of oysters, 6d. In this war- time, during which the cost of food has so enormously increased, it makes one's mouth water to read in these same accounts that a quarter of mutton was only is., five chickens 6d., half a fat lamb 2s. 6d., a goose 5d., a fat pig 2s. My word!' And my breakfast bacon costs me is. sd. per pound. I fancy I hear a gentle voice from the direction of Burtonwciod saying "More fool, you." Ah, well, such knowledge is too wonderful for me; I cannot attain unto it. To return to the point. It strike' me that the price of fish so long ago was pretty high. One has to bear in mind that in those fine old times a working man earned only from 2d. to 6d. a day in wages, so that he would hardly be tempted to indulge in a surf3it even of herrings at Id. each.

Leather.

The usual quick run of trade was easily maintained during the week (says the War- rington correspondent of the "Leather Trades Review "), without any slackening of inquiry, which still remains very much in excess of the available supply, and the great grumble all the time with consumers is that deliveries are so slow in coming along, and when they do arrive are of such small proportions; on the other hand it is almost as great a worry ,to the tanner to know how best to divide out his pro- duction so that each at least shall have a turn. Values all round continue their upward move- ment, though in the absence of any general quotations it is impracticable to give a. definite list, business to-day being largely a matter of Independent sales, and a repeat, as a rule, is only at an enhanced value. Forward business there is practically none, and, candidly, neither seller nor buyer is particularly anxious to engage far ahead.

USEFUL SPARE GROUND.

Latchford Clergyman Suggests Whole- sale Growing Of Vegetables. In order to minimise theeffeet of the increase in prices of food generally, the Rev. G. C. Briggs, at the annual meeting of the Latchford; Garden Society on Wednesday evening, thought it would be useful if that association could urge upon a people in the district the urgency of cultivating every bit of spare land of which they had control. Prices, he said, were going up by leaps and bounds, and they could be quite certain that they would not come down for a considerable time. It-was the duty of everyone who had any spare ground to cultivate it with vegetables— (hear, hear)—and if the society could do some educational work on thqse lines they would .be doing very excellent service for the country. In Germany every spare plot as covered with potatoes or other vegetables. With all their faults—and they had many—Germans were a far-seeing and clever people, and in that respect we should emulate them.

The report .of the Secretaries (Messrs. G. Hooper and J. S. Burgess) showed that there had been a loss on the year's working, although they still had a balance in hand of XS 17s. 4d. A good many members of the society had on- listed, but the results of the 1915 show were very encouraging. •

Not only had the war upset affairs, but on the show. day they had unpropitious weather. It was very difficult nowadays to get interested in any subject other than that of war, and perhaps it was most difficult of all to get fresh subscriptions. Of course, there should be put forth every effort on behalf, of the war, and they made no complaint in that respect. They could only hope and pray that by this time next year peace would have beer, declared, and then he .was sure the usefulness of the society would be apparent. Carter & on Ids

BRIDGE STREET AA IRRINGTON.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR YOUR

POULTRY AND CHICK FEED, SPRATT'S, OLD CALABAR, ENTWISTLE'S, ARMITAGE'S,

HEARSON'S INCUBATOR.

•

I am not reproaching those who are trying to do what they think is the best in advancing the science of economical poultry rearing, says a "Feathered World" writer, but I do think the time has come for a warning not to carry this pressure too far, but to pave and think. It must be the very sanest of policies to remember if we are to take the world lead in poultry and eggs that our future stock must be sturdily built stuff, birds of the very finest type to breed from, well built up, and capable of breeding the layers of the future, to last and have sound health. I could go on with useful evidence showing why our eggs are smaller. Here is a hint. If you wish to increase the size of your eggs, breed from hens two years and upwards, and never from 'millets, not properly developed, in their first year, because, in addition to laying their eggs, they have in turn to build and grow their frames as well. and they are not ready to breed from until they have completely and fully developed all their bodies and organs for work. Get a good matured yearling cockerel and two- year-old hens from the strongest money can buy. Then you are on right lines for business and will get value for your money.

The Halfpenny Egg. Captain Webber, says the " Yorkshire Observer," is a very _sanguine man if he really believes that eggs can be profitably produced at one halfpenny each. He said in a lecture that the person who could keep twelve hens properly should get :4,800 eggs a year. One must get the right sort*of hens, rid oneself of the wasters, and give the birds ":water,:air, grit, exercise and sympathy." One, comments the "Poultry World," is afraid that no amount of sympathy would make amends for the absence of corn and other bought foods, the present prices of which rule the halfpenny egg out of court. lint does the producer really want eggs to he produced at a halfpenny each? We doubt it.

Overcoming the Insect Trouble. If chickens are to be reared successfully, and if they are to grow satisfactorily, they must be kept clean_ If they are carelessly looked after and are allowed to become infested with insects, &c., their growth is seriously retarded, and they are very liable to be unhealthy. With newly. hatched chickens it is a good plan to anoint their heads as soon as they are dry with a prepara- tion composed of the following :—loz. mercurial ointment, loz. lard, loz. sulphur, and sufficient paraffin oil to make a semi fluid. This process should be repeated when they are about three weeks ,or a •,Anonth, old, for there is no more effective way of keeping down vermin. When they are older they must be dusted with a disin- fectant powder, particular attention being paid to the feathers under the wings and on the back, for it is on these parts that the vermin mostly congregate. " Modifications" of Nature.

A stimulating diet will often make hens very prolific even when their inherent tendency does not in any way favour heavy egg production. For instance, if I take a hen that was hatched, we will say, early in April, and feed her on a very forcing diet, I shall no doubt get plenty of winter eggs and a very good yearly average. My feeding is such that ft frees the yolk secretion, and causes the oocytes to become more rapidly charged with yolk than I should have otherwise expected. But what I want you most particu- larly to note is that this kind of heavy egg pro- duction is purely the result of a very stimulat- ing diet, and that is not due to a high fecund inheritance transmitted by the parents of such a bird. It is therefore what I am going to call, an acquired modification. Modifications of this kind are n9t of an inheritable nature, and such a hen would not breed any better stock birds as a result of her " acquired " heavy production. She can, transmit only what she inherits; she did, not inherit a high fecund factor; otherwise she would have laid well (just. because she could not help it) without all this forcing diet. The extra meat and pea meal and poultry spice I gave her just stimulated her egg organs and had no effect whatever upon her germ-cells; unfortunately, it is her germ-cells which she transmits to her progeny, and not pea meal, meat meal, and poultry spice, nor, be it 'noted, any effects that these three things may have had upon the mother's egg production.

If I have tWo hens, the inherent potential egg production of which is 100 eggs per year each, and from one I obtained 175 eggs in year as a result of stimulating feeding, and from the other I obtain only the potential 100 as a result of perfectly natural feeding, I should expect both of these hens to breed me exactly the same class of chickens, and I should not expect the extra 75 eggs obtained by the use of a stimulating diet on the first hen to be we'll, so much as a rush candle to me so far as her breeding value was concerned. The hen I like for breeding me high fecund stock is the hen that lays heavily through the winter, when my feeding is calculated to de- crease rather than to increase egg production. If a hen lays well on a diet that is .anything but one calculated to promote egg production T think i can safely conclude that her high fecundity is due to inherent high fecund factors. and is not IT the slightest dogr.o due to any modifying influences.—" The Pbultry World."

LAMPS USED BY FARMERS AND SHEPHERDS

The attention of the President of the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries has been drawn to the possibility of farmers and shepherds, by carrying lights 'at night when attending stock, rendering themselves liable to prosecution under the lighting regulations at present in force. Lord Selborne has been in communication, with the Home Office, and is advised that the sole requirement is that such lights must be pro- perly screened. WOMEN AS FARM HANDS.

Too much respect.. must not be paid to the opinion of farmers who condemn 'women workers when trying to get exemptions from military service for their men.. In cases it may be that women have failed to reach the ideal—but men have been known to fail, too.

Asked by the Woking Tribunal if he could not get women to take his places a pig-breeder said he would rather give up that part of his business altogether, as women would want more lookiqg after than the pigs themselves.

A poultry farmer, applying at Chertsey for the exemption of a male employe, to assist:with the incubators and young chickens, said he had tried female labour with disastrous results. A farmer seeking to retain.his last employe to assist with over a hundred cattle and pigs said he had paid a woman kl weekly, but when the wet weather came she departed.

So far, it is said, only 14,000 women have answered the call to fill the places of the 200,000 agricultural labourers who lave enlisted.;‘

"A SCHOOLGIRL'S APPEAL."

The Board of Agriculture lies paid a high compliinent to .a schoolgirl of ,Chureh Stretton, pupil at the Girls' High .School, Shrewsbury A well-known member of the Salop County Council offered prizes for competition among pupils of the county secondary schools for the best essays dealing with the question of agricul- tural work for women, and Miss Eilidh Hay Forbes' essay was selected for publication. This "schoolgirl's appeal" has now appeared in the " Journal " of the Board of Agriculture, and is distributed in the form of a leaflet. Miss Forbes, says "Farm and Home," who writes well, throws contempt on the suggestion that there is anything derogatory to women in field work. It has somewhat surprised many people who in these democratic days had held the view not only that all labour was ennobling, but that idleness was an offence against good citizenship, to find that numbers of women belonging to the so-called working classes really consider that any work outside their own houses is "degrad- ing." No doubt the explanation is that gener- ally women do not enter on such work unless there is need for it, and this need implies poverty; and the open avowal of poverty is held to imply loss of self-respect. We May agree with the copy-book maxims that dispute the last Assumption, but the fact remains, that the con-. of 'narrow means.has long been ono of the most essential of objects in the eyes of "respectability." One of the few good results. of this unhappy war is the growth of saner ' views on this matter. In towns, ladies who would never have thought of carrying parcels may now be seen taking home their purchases, and no one is ashamed of admitting that the war has brought about a state of comparative " hard-uppedness "; thousands of housewives who kept maids are perforce doing without them, and men whose time is paid for highly in the City may be seen at factories in their spare time, with coats doffed or overalls on, doing work for which the pay is a few pence per hour That the British country-woman should there- fore be restrained from offering her services on the farm by the idea that the work entails loss of self-respect..is absurd. and it is cheering that this VOIMg schoolgirl, who doubtless'reffects the views of her own class, should have put so well hefore the women who we hone will read her little essay. not only the need for the call which is made to them, but the real dignity and beauty of the work which they are invited to do.

F 1 NANCES.

The financial account rendered by Mr. Adeane at the last meeting of the Council of the "Royal" shows that the Society is in a good position. In 1905 the Society's capital stood at .£2.6?8 t to-day, allowing for its writing down by £10,823, it stands ,at £47.031, and in ddition a sum of £7,333 is invested in War Loan stock for the purposes of insurances' of the staff, making a total of £54,364. The estimated receints for 1916 (other than in respect of the show) are put at £9.850, and the expenditure at £8,671, leaving a balance of £1.179. IN THE GARDEN.

POTATOES.

I have just been looking through a catalogue of potatoes, and have been astonished to find the number of varieties listed. More so-called new varieties or varieties with new names are being added to the already too long list of potatoes than to that of any other vegetable. Many of us might safely grow one third of the varieties of various vegetables and still have plenty for the table, but what, ,I ask, would happen if. one were to grow one-third of the potatoes now catalogued? No doubt in many cases improved varieties have been raised, but many, after hav- ing tried•them, find that they do no good. The soil is an important factor in the vallie of th.x potato. Take the variety Langworthy as an instance of this. A friend of mine who superin- tends a garden in the south of Scotland tried this last year, and found 'it was useless .in his district, the crop being so poor that it was a waste of land to ‘..row it. The quality Was fairly good, but the crop was poor in. the ex- treme. New varieties are certificated, but these are only dealt with as grown in one particular soil. Transfer them to a totally different soil and it is just possible that they will be wanting in the two most important essentials, viz., crop- ping and. quality. Given a favourable season they may be first rate, while in the opposite they may be of no value' whatever. Many of these so-called improvements require special

culture and a certain kihd of soil to do them well, and that, probably, ii the reason why growers who have not the advantage of the required soil fail to succeed with them.

THE WINTER JASMINE.

In country cottage. gardens this. harbinger of spring usually finds a place, and anyone who desires to use the sprays in the house should cut them about a foot long before the buds come •Out: Put these .into a deep vase with plenty of water, and every bud will open: much larger and finer.then 'those in the open air. When the flower buds are about half open a pieee of the stem may be cut off to keep up the succession of bloom. Thus treated, I find that in a room where fire is only occasionally used a succession of flowers will be kept up for a fortnight.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
PLANTING FRUIT TREES.

No doubt many of my readers have been un- able to plant their fruit trees. It .is not too late to do this, but there are one or two points that those.: who receive _their trees from the nursery .shoukl not neglect.- As the roots, when the trees- come.to lukad, are very often dry, the first thing to be done is to...soak the.m in a tub of water for an hour or two. This applies to shrubs and roses as well as-fruit trees. .So soon as the, roots have iJartially dried after having been removed from the water' they should be carerfully gone over with -a sharp knife, any of the jagged and bruised ends cut off, and any bruises anywhere about them pared. .All the holes for the tree:l. ,-hould,have been got ready so that theY. can be planted atone.' Each hole should be at the very least a food wider than is the spread of the roots. The hOles should be on the shallow side rather than deep—indeed, if the .roots .near the stem, more especially in an unkindly soil, be covered two inches or three inches that Will be itinple.:- Roots naturally go deep, and it is well to keep them near the sur face at the outset.- If mulched with rotten manure theY can then be kept there. In plant- ing spread the roots ciut very carefully and fill ,in round them with some fine soil so as to encourage a good start. Then gradually add the natural soil and tread all very firmly. Should the weather be dry, ii.t4 often happens in March. • give them a thorough soaking of Water to settle the soil. It is well, too, in the case of standards to put a ,stake to each tree.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."'

Broadmindedness Displayed At Guardians Give Dr. Sephton An The Bradford Meeting. Appointment.

By the. Rev. H. OGDEN (Delegate from Warrington.)

The delegates to the Bradford meetings fore- gathered in a shower of alert, which has con- almost without ce6sation until now. ===== 0002601_19160311_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
HEARTBREAKING WORK,

The Conference was on the tiptoe of expect- ancy when the President rose on Tuesday morn- I 1 z to deliver his Presidential address. It is impossible to do justice in any brief report to the statesmanlike outline of the President. nistorically the origin of the divisions in Free Churches was traced, but as' the President said, "Unfortunately division cannot stop just when we have had enough," but stop division We must if the Free Church is to accomplish its destiny. With line pathos there was described to the assembly the heartbreaking work of the villages in which Free Churches were competi- tors With each other, and where more efficiency Slid more enduring labour could be accom- plished by one pastor who had charge of the Whole Free Church work of the village. Country towns (Warrington. with .so many Free Churches in. the centre of .the town, was present in one's mind) couLd be more efficiently worked if only there was a more sensible adjustment of Free Church- forces. The President finally outlined- . a skeleton scheme, which he suggested us a workable basis of the first conference, a seheme that on some subsequent occasion ought to he outlined in your valuable paper. The whole assembly rose in one spontaneous mass to: cheer the President and endorse his scheme. Difficulties., grave and great, are entailed in the scheme, hut the fervent prayers of Free Churchmen are that all difficelties will -1)e. surmounted, and. the President's efforts crowned with success.

"A PERSONAL MATTER."

At last the game of shuttle-cock which the Board of Guardians have been playing over the appointment of a medical officer for No. 3 Dis- trict, and -public vaccinator of the Rixton trict, has come to an end, for at their meeting on Wednesday they appointed -Dr. Sephton to succeed his late father in those positions. They made the appointment, however, with- out having read the letters from the two appli- ca,nts—Dr. Sephton and Dr. Bennett. It was desired by some members that these should be read, but Mr. J. W. Knowles said he regarded it as a waste of time and eventually it was decided by a vote not to read them.

Mr. John Ball proposed that the appointments should go to Dr. Sephton, adding that the bulk of the people in the district knew him and wanted him to continue as their medical man. He had had experience of the work, having helped his late father. ItEr,,K.nowles, seconding, expressed the opinion that-where they found people's ideas running in one particular way, it was their duty to try and please them, so long as it was not costing any more, and so long as the service was efficient. That was the reason he supported the appoint- ment of Dr. Septiton.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
Unread Letters.

Mr. W. C. Brooks considered it was hardly fair to the applicants, that their letters should not be read, as the position had been advertised for. 'There was nothing but a bias of opinion in regard to the appointments, and he would neither vote for nor agAinst. Mr. flail: There is a bias for a certain reason:

_ Mr. Brooks: If the Local Government Board were to hear that, I do not think they would adopt the recommendation. The appointment of Dr. Bennett was then ,proposed by Mr. Thomas Dale, who said the Board had considered the convenience of the poor sick people who had to be served in that district, and, in view of the promise Dr. Bennett had made that if 'appointed to the positions he would open a surgery in Croft, his nomination was the better of the two. Dr. Bennett would be able to look after the poor--= Mr. Ball: And the other people as well. Mr. Dale: Yes, but the sick poor in particu- lar. He considered Dr: Bennett to be the superior man of the two.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
A Personal Matter.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell straightaway de— clared, in seconding, that there were some mem- bers of the Board who only seemed to .be, pres- ent when there was some apsointment in the air in which they were interested. lie suggested the appointment of Dr. Sephton was merely the desire of certain gentlemen to console because he had not been appointed medical officer of health. The gentleman who had pro- posed Dr. .Sephton had candidly admitted that with him-it was a personal matter. Mr. Ball: Yes. . _ _

Rey. A: M. Mitchell: Now we have hit the right nail on the head. We should respect sympathy and friendship--- • Mr. Bail: They were bothlriends of mine. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: But we here should act not from personal motives, but in the best interests of the people. Personal considerations should be avoided in- a; Public appointment. What they ought to do was to make up their minds who was the better man to aerie the town- ship and poor people. By offering to start a surgery in Croft, Dr. Bennett would be greatly appreciated. Mr. J. A. Billinge regarded the qualifications of Dr. Sepliton as being minor. He believed the better appointment to be that of Dr. Ben- nett.,

On the amendnient being put, three voted for and thirteen against, and then the Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) formally moved, end Mr. Ball seconded, the anpointment of Dr. Sephton, which, on the appeal of the Chairman, was car- ried, if not 'unanimously, without dissent.

SHOULD WAGES BE

" POOLED " ?

Clergyman Suggests Workers Should Help The Fighters.

FENNINGS For Children Cutting their Teeth. Prevent Convulsions. Are Cooling and Soothing. Contain nothing injurious to a tender babe.

Bold Everryyre Starnpsd B3r.s, is. 3d. and 35., with Fait _Directions. Safe 111.111 Easy Teething' Teething'

READ FENNINGS' EVERT MOTHER'S BOOK.' It contains Valuable !Hata on Feeding, • Teething, Weaning, Sleeping. etc. aor your Cbengst or Geoce.r far a **KEE _copy, ar gas via be sent, POST Plait:,a appliceumt to ALSRLD FE.Bsoics„ Chele ef Wigbe.

(Alona hare the Trade Ma,*: 'A BstsT IN A, CIL&DLIC.") e

• The Rev. A. M. Mitchell, in the " Burtonwood Parish Magazine" for March, draws attention to the question of pooling, and suggests that war workers at home who are making abnormal wages should do something for the soldiers who are missing their wages, but risking their lives. " If," he asks, "Cabinets should pool—what may be said about colliers (other workers may be interested though possibly not to the same extent as colliers)? It is well known, in colliery districts, at least, that miners at many pits are earning- fabulous wages. "Thinking this over, the unequal state of things comes .home td us with all the force of conviction. The case as it is presented to us stands thus. _ _

"Large drafts of men have passed from th 4 pits to Military Service, not the least satisfac- tory workers, average workers on the whole some better, no doubt, some worse. The majority of these men only receive is. Id. a day: dependents, of course, receive the fixed allow- ances, with an allotment out of the soldier's pay. "The man at home. who, because he has bided a wee is now 'starred' as indispensable to the coal industry, works, it is net disputed, long and hard, netting it may be as Much as .E 4 a week—something anyhow not far short of double of his normal earnings, . . But is it fair for the men at home, however hard' they may work, to grow rich in such circumstances, is .it fair- to their fellow workmen now in khaki, that there should be nothing for them? " A Nice Little--Sum."

"It is not perhaps too late to inquire if some- thing cannot be done whilst the excessively high wages are being received to pool a part of this vast weekly sum for the benefit of the men who now 'are riskihg -Oath-lives for hcime and Country. so that on their'return they•May,find something less Cold and more reliable than charity to fall back Upon. If all earnings in mines, workshops Sze., over ft certain agreed upon 'amount -were pooled a nice little sum would be available to give our brave fellows a fresh start when peace has been proclaimed and ' Tommy ' has. come marching home again. " A patriotic worker will not quarrel with such a suggestion. He will welcome it. . An understanding of :this kind would be infinitely •preferable to the taxation of wages, which is certain to come if, something in the pooling way does not take place. Better a year or two of business-like, systematic pooling than a cycle of Iwage taxatiun."

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Women's Hospital in Euston-road. The Queen recently visited the Women's Hos- pital in the Euston road where she spent a considerable time in the ward given over to soldiers. This hospital, staffed and managed by women, has several unusual features, one of which is a circular ,ward, in which the feet of the patients converge towards the centre. It is claimed for this shaped ward that it is more hygienic, being without :corners, also that it permits of more sociability than the con- ventional ward with its straight line of beds. Last, but not least, each patient has the f benefit of the windows, which are placed all round the room. The kitchens, as would be expected in a Women's Hospital, are Ivo,' ?Is • efficiency. Women's ingenuity has here been cxerciseu to th,,3 full, and the result is a nuiLtln of clever devices for saving time and labour.

Women Army Cooks. When first the idea of women cooks for the Army was mooted', many objections were raised, the principal being that women could not go to the Front, but in view of the many advantages such a scheme offered, it was decided to begin with one or two encampments. The experiment has ' succeeded 'admirably,- and effects a wt.: considerable saving of money. The women take more interest in the work than men to provide variety, and well-prepared food. It is therefore possible that women will replace men in a great many of the encampments, thus releasing quite a large number for active service. After the war, too, it is possible that women will retain their footing in Army kitchens, and that a very considerable saving of food and money will be thus effected. Women Dentists.

Excellent prospects await duly qualified women dentists, and 'the present opportunity for training at the Royal Dental Hospital in Lon- don will doubtless be seized by many girls seek- ing a remunerative occuna lion. For some years past the National Dental Hospital has admitted women students, but the concession in the case of the former institution is quite recent. The Lqndon School of Medicine (for women) has arranged to work with both hospitals, and' course of instruction is now being drawn up, which will occupy a minimum period of four years, and include three examinations. Those women who are not attracted by the extraction and filling of teeth, may find the mechanical side of dentistry more interesting, and in that also there are now good prospects. Very few skilled mechanics succeed in 'making good plates for artificial tem b. for whinh there is a great demand. Tbe work is well §uited to women. it is also well paid, and is less exacting than that of an operating dentist. A Souvenir For New Zealanders.

A most interesting function took place recently in London, when eighty wounded New Zealanders, who had taken part in the unfor- tunate Gallipoli campaign, were to return tc., the Dominion, and several ladies desired that they should take with them a souvenir from the

Motherland. It was decided that a silken Union Jack mounted on an oaken staff would form the most suitable gift. Accordingly in this short time, the money, was collected, and the flag s beautifully embroidered. A magnificent shield inscribed "To the New Zealand heroes. from the women of England," was slung in one corner in black and white silk cords—the colours of the colony. Beneath was a laurel wreath with " Gallipo " enclosed. Each man was pre- sented with a tiny replica of the flag which will be placed in the Dominion Parliament House.

Dearer Silks. Silk, like most other fabrics, is sure to rise in price in the near future, for the crops of the raw material are this year much below the average, added ,to which there is a shortage of of dyestuffs, and the difficulties in the matters of labouii and transport. To buy silk now for use later on will not prove a mistake. Some of the prettiest of evening dresses for young girls are made of ivory white and pale buff crepe, and. with them are worn scarves of brilliant-hued tulle, arranged in a number of ways on the shoulders, and round the neck. A border of tiny porcelaine beads makes a pretty finish to such a scarf. An accessory—the return of which will be welcomed—is the fichu of the cape or pelerine variety. In white georgette, ninon, chiffon or muslin, made with a double cape, and having deep hems, it may be relied upon to transform, and to bring, up-to-date an old- fashioned evening gown of silk or satin. Book muslin of early Victorian' days is to be used this summer, for many of these &hug, as well as for collars and cuffs. Its disadvantage lies in its tendency to crumple and to lose its stiffness. New Underwear.

Petticoats become increasingly important as the days lengthen. They are fuller and still fuller, and as they will be required to keep the summer wide skirts from-clinging to the ankles, many of the newest are furnished with numer- ous rows of tiny frills gathered on to the foundation—giving a crinoline effect to a silk or crepe skirt. Piping cords at the top and bottom of gaugings is another device adopted for stiffening'. Many evening petticoats are ex- ceedingly beautiful, the embroideries and laces most profusely used in their adornment being of the most exquisite fineness and delicacy. The old fashioned lingerie petticoats are to have a vogue this summer, to the great satisfaction of the makers, who suffered severely during thp reign of the tube skirt. Combined undergar- ments were never more popular than at present the camisole--also an important item in the present day feminine wardrobe, is generally formed in one with the pettrcoat, either with a narrow band to connect the two, or as in the Princess shape. The kimono sleeve is seen on undergarments of all kinds, but many camisoles are sleeveless.

Fashions For bhildren. Children's clothes always reflect the modes of their elders, consequently the little people are much more simply dressed than was the case a few years ago, but their clothes are pretty and decidedly practical. A typical outdoor garment is cut in one piece from neck to hem, and is very full, a very loose belt of the material mark- ing the waist line. The coat covers up the dress -onapletely, its only trimming the buttons, which begin at the base of the throat and Anish a t the hem.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
SUGGESTI ONS.

Mr DEAR CHILDREN, My request that you should suggest a sub- ject for our competition this week has proved a great success. Help has come from all hands. /t has been pleasing to see how anxious my little people are to dc.r all they can for their country in its time of trial. Your letters are full of patriotism, just as they should be at the present time—although some have chosen ordinary sub- jects, which are also good. After a great deal of thinking I have decided to give the prize to Nephew Robert Smart (689), who proposes the following subject for next week :--

The Most Wonderful Invention From 1815 To

Now, and Who Invented It.

Essays on that subject, as Robert says, will show that the British people "can invent things." That is true. We hear a great deal of how superior the Germans are to us in many things, but people who really study the doings of their own country know that we have done infinitely more for the advancement of the world than any other people, including the Germans, whose chief occupation has been to live on other people's brains. So we will have for the competition:next week an essay on in- ventions and inventors since 1815. , Nephew Robert Smart lives at 41, Price-street, and attends Ileathside School. I am especially pleased to see how the boys have rallied this week. They have been outrun badly by the girls lately, but they have done much better this week. I shall in future select subjects occasionally from the other suggestions sent in, so that those who feel they could write lipon them- will have a chance of doing so. Your loving UNCLE JACIt.

has served with honour and distinction from one war period to another during six reigns—those of George 111., George IV., William IV., Victoria, Edward VII. and George V.—contributing materially all the time to the health, freshn.ers, and appearance of the men of the service and of the general public Always include Pears in your Parcels to the Front

and to it we must give ourselves heart and soul. It means a loyal adherence to all our work and to all our worship. We at home must 'prepare ourselves and get ready for the work which lies in front of us. We must get into such a con- dition of spiritual activity that when the flood of 'humanity comes back in our midst they must find a living faith and a living Church, on lii-e with the love of God. No Christian minister has any right in the pulpit of this country at this moment who is not concerned with the positive work of the Christitin Church. • I tell you quite honestly I would not have conic back if I could not have conceived a positive work for the Christian Church at the present moment.

I said it was with feelings of joy and gratitude that they welcomed Air. James back in their midst, at the end of a second period of five months' service with the troops in France. They . would like to place on record their high appreciation of his aetion in volunteering to undertake this work wit ,k the Y.M.C.A. amongst our brave lads at the Front. ,They knew it had been at considerable self-sacrifice not only to himself, but to Mrs. lames, who had endeared herself to their hearts by t he cheerful manner in which she helped in the work of the church during her husband's absence. It had been a. great pleasure for the members of the Church _ to hear from time to time encouraging reports of the splendid work Mr. James had accom- plished in France. They had had those reports from men on the spot, who were capable of judg- ing of the value of that work. As a Church, they felt it was an honour to participate in some degree in. helping the splendid work the Y.M.C.A, are carrying on wherever our troops are to ,be found. It had been a loyal sacrifice to them to lose the services of Mr. James for a period of five months, but such a sacrifice had been far and away counter-balanced by the knowledge. of the splendid work Nl'r. James had accomplished on • the other side. (Applause.) --Mr. Josiah Longland, on behalf of the con- gregation, said that daring the time Mr. James was away he-had' never been absent from their thoughts, and as each Sunday passed they had looked forward to his return. inc deacons had provided them, no doubt, with, good substitutes, but after all it was uot quite like the real thing. Although they put up with it, he wanted Mr. James to understand , dot in, welcoming him back they were not giving him permission to go again. (Laughter anti ~pplause ) The sacrifice which the Church had made was net quite real- ised by everyone. No Church or congregation coul go- on unless there was someone, at the helm.' Although they had some efficient men an ongst the deacons, they felt tnat they wanted a' pastor. He did.not agree with those Free Churches who thought they could do without a pastor; personally, le: p,eferred to know the man who was going to speak 'to him. Mrs.• 1 James had fulfilled her ~art very well whilst her [ husband had been av,- (Applause.) Faith In T he Future. The Rev. T. T. James, in acknowledging the welcome, said he wanted sinceie.,;, w thank the speakers for the kind words they had said about him, not that he deserved them. Such a wel- come home made him feel it was the real thing. It was a great thing to be back at home, and at Wycliffe Church, and to be back in one's own country. It was a great thing also to be at a tea like they had had that night. If they had lived on tinned meat, tinned milk, tinned butter, and tinned this; that, and the other, for a long time, they would be glad to get some ordinary civilised kind of food. (Hear, hear, and laugh- 'ter.) He had felt all along that the sacrifice on the part of the Church had been very real in- deed. It seemed to him to be quite impossible that any Church could for a long period do with- out feeling the inconvenience. He knew they had felt it, but by their work and faithfulness they had done all they could to minimise that inconvenience as much as possible. He trusted that such a gathering would be a time of en- couragement for all of them, and to look for- ward in the future with faith. Good service , had been rendered to them by one who was the minister of the church for quite a long time— their friend, Mr. Yonge. They were grateful to him, and their thanks were also due to his col- league, Mr. Pitt, for all the encouraging service he had given, making no fuss about things, but doing whatever he could with that willingness, geniality and goodness of heart which Were the mark of all he did. (Applause.) He felt it had been a sacrifice for his dear wife, and he wished to thank them for having done ill they could to cheer her. He believed she had felt their real friendship, which had helped her very much. -Her messages to him were a help and inspira- tion whilst he was away. . They were uncertain about the future outlook. but he was certain that notwithstanding its un- certainty and difficulty the officers and members of the Church Were'. prepared to face it with brave hearts. He trusted they -would feel that these were not so much difficult days, as days which presented them with a special and re- markable opportunity—that all difficulties were opportunities—and that when a thing was hard there was something real for therb to do. Any Church could keep going easily when everything was in its favour; it was when they were up against something trying that they could put themselves to the test. The Church which laced a difficult situation bravely and with good heart came out of it a stronger and better Church than it was before. He hoped that in these days of special events—days when the national and individual burdens alike were heavy—were days of great opportunities for their Church and for their Christian life. (Applause.) In conclusibn, he again thanked them for their warm welcome home. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160311_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
Religion Near The Guns.

The Church may find these in many ways days of great difficulty; it may even discover incon- venience at the present moment in darkened streets and other restrictions. I would remind you. of the history Of Free Church worship. It was made by men and women who met in barns, and in caves of the earth, and who expected at any moment to be dragged from the altar to 1.11.• fire and the stake. Our inconveniences shrink to nothing in the light of historical experience. I have seen our lads at the Front stand bare- headed in the snow and storm, within rango,of the guns, and pray. for those they love at home There are boys to-day taking the body and blood of the Lord in Holy Communion, with the elements spread on the bare, bleak ground of the battlefield. And the simple and positive thing asked of us at home is that we be true to our God, and our Lord, and our Church, and keep the fire of spiritual reality alight, and the flame of faith and truth :unquenched, until we are all with each other again to build the King- dom of God in the better days that are to be.

P.S.A. ANNIVERSARY COINCIDES WITH RETURN.

It was an interesting coincidence that the -reverend gentleman's return should coincide with the celebration of the 23rd anniversary of the Wycliffe P.5..A., of which he is the presi- dent, and on Saturday evening, when a tea table conference was held in the church parlour, a hearty welcome was extended him.

Mr. B. Farrar presided, and among those present were Alderman W. G, Wilkins (presi- dent of the National Federation of Brother- hoods) and the Revs. W. Reid, Percy meson, IT. Ogden and C. Pitt. The Welcome.

The Chairman said that as a Brotherhood we had much to be thankful for. God had been good to us for 23 years, and many living and many departed had testified to the blessings of our meetings, and had been led to a Consecra- tion to God. •

Alderman Wilkins Gelivered an interesting address, and those specially concerned in offer- ing thanks to him were the Rev. W. Reid and the Rev. H. Ogden. Mr. W. Beswick and Mr. A. Healey voiced the P.S.A.'s welcome to Mr. James. Mr. James briefly replied, and the Chairman said he would feel all the stronger for having Mr. James again amongst them. There was a large meeting at the P.S.A. on Sunday afternoon. The President presided, and an address was delivered by Alderman Wilkins. The Salvation Army Band rendered selections, and Mr. Jos. Poole sang "Consider the lilies. ' The Hon. Secretary (Mr. Farrar) welcomed Private Horrocks, was was present, and he asked the audience to show by acclamation their hearty welcome to Mr. James. Eleven new members were enrolled.

BACK FROM THE FRONT.

Rev. T. T. James Resumes His Pastorate At Wycliffe Church.

After an absence of several months, the Rev. T. T. James has returned full of health from Flanders, where he has been ministering to our soldiers. All those whom he has come in contact with across the Channel bear testimony to his high character, and the zeal with which he has carried out his duties as a chaplain has proved most helpful. The reverend gentleman arrived on Saturday and spent a busy week-end at Wycliffe Congre- gational Church, of which he is the pastor.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GREAT' SPIRITUAL BATTLE WAR HAS EVOLVED.

"I would not have come back if I could not have conceived a positive work for the Christian Church at the present moment," was a striking passage in the sermon of the Rev. T. T. James, at the Wyclifie Congregational Church on Sunda, evening, when he resumed his pastorate after a second period with the Y.M.C.A. amongst the troops in France. The " reunion " of pastor and congregation had been joyfully anticipated, and the services on Sunday marked the love and loyalty in which the pastor is held by members and friends of the Church. The evening service was one of special intercession, when prayer was offered for those members of the Church and its kindred organisations whO are on service with the Forces.

DEFINITE SUBJECTS FOR PRAYER. Mr. James, in his address, said he would like to suggest some definite things which might be the subject of their prayer. At that service they remembered by name those who were away on service, and there were two things in connection with them which they might well bear in mind. Of course, they were all very really concerned about their physical safety, but it was rather on the spiritual side that he was thinking that night. First of all they might ask that it might be given them to preserve a new and living spiritual faith. They would not be surprised if he said that out there at the Front, where the machinery of warfare went on perpetually, doing its destructive task, where human life was apparently so cheap: where a man began a day and never knew whether he might finish it, where the individual was seem- ingly so insignificant among the countless masses, it was not easy for anyone to 'keep fast hold of the belief that what happened to him was a matter of much moment in the sight of God. Easy enough it might be to believe in some vast force concerned with the destiny of nations, or some mighty power bound up with the rise and fall of kingdoms, but to believe in a living, personal God, to whom the well-being of the individual soul and the experiences of the single life were Matters of eternal momenr, was one of the greatest spiritual battles that any lad had had to fight. To Sustain "The Lads."

And second, continued Mr. James, we need to pray that lads out in the rough and tumble of active military service may be sustained in their effort to witness to the reality of their Christian discipleship. There is a great deal happening across at the Front in the way of real religious influence, but it is misleading to speak about it as though to find yourself there is to find your- self in the midst of some wonderful religious revival. That religious feeling is often true and deep, and that religious experience has come to many men by way of the strange and often terrible happenings around them, is perfectly true, but equally true it is that for many a lad this adventure to foreign parts has meant a contact with temptation and moral evil not known before. You must conceive how it has meant the removal of the -restraints of home, the removal of the. -restraints of church and, school, the sudden disappearance of that society of good and pure' womanhood which means so much for all of us, and the sudden plunging into a life entirely new in its surroundings and temptations. And then you must pray above and beyond the preservation from physical I harm which we all desire for thOse we love, there may be granted them that unseen power hich keeps the soul of manhood pure and clean.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
The Call to the Church.

Ono other thing, and this has to do with those at home. We must ask that we may be able in the Church of Jesus Christ to conceive our work at this moment from the positive stand- point. We are not here merely to mark time, or to hug our own souls, or to make ourselves as comfortable as possible until this conflict is over. We are not hero merely to hang on and keep things going somehow until things get easier. ' The Church of Jesus Christ is to con- ceive its work at this immediate moment as one of supreme importance. By and by millions of men will be returning tO this country after their strange experiences abroad. All the young men of the coming years almost will be those who have passed through the fires of this un- paralleled catastrophe. They will be demanding something ,from the country they have served. they will be demanding something from our homes, they will be demanding something from the Churches of our land. It is the supreme business of the Church at this moment to be facing this fact, to be so looking to the vitality its ,faith and the vigour of its spiritnal life, that amongst us those who return may find an Atmosphere which will be the making of the future. This is the positive task of the Church

The six Prudential Approved Societies formed under the National Insurance Act, 1911, continue to make satisfactory progress, and the valuable ser- vices rendered to the members by the Agency Staff are highly appreciated. It is .with feelings of pride and satisfaction that the Directors are able to report that no fewer than 0,221 of their staff are either serving with the Colours or have attested or been rejected for ser- vice: 1.305 from the indoor staff and 7,9111 from the outdoor staff.

The values of Stock Exchange securities are determined, under the Articles of Association of the Company, by the Directors. Due allowance has been made for accrued interest, and the book value of these securities as set forth in the Balance Sheet stands considerably below cost price. A careful investigation as to the actual saleable value on 31st December.l9ls (all minimum price quotations being greatly reduced), compared with the book value, shows that the Investments Reserve Funds are much more than sufficient to meet any depreciation of the permanent securities. Terminable securities have been valued on a basis which. with Sinking Funds already established, provides for the equalisa- tion of the book values and the redemption values at the date of maturity.

Some excitement Was caused in Thelwall Church on Sunday night during the service by some paper shading the light becoming ignited. Pity it wasn't a Zeppelin!. Those are the burnt sacrifices we like.

I have heard of an interesting meeting. A soldier who had been, in Thelwall Heys Con- valescent Home for several months was sur- prised at the beginning of the week to see his brother hobble into the same room as a patient. The latter had been at the Lord Derby Hospitai at Winwick for a considerable time, and it was mere luck that he should have been drafted to Thelwall. Neither brother had heard of each other for a year. They went to the Front, at the opening of the war.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MARCH 11, 1916.

EARLIER CLOSING OF SHOPS AND MARKET.

Seldom has a suggestion met with such general approval 28 the one we made last week, that the tradesmen of the town should .comply with the present requirements of the situation by closing their shops when day- light gives out. Nearly all agree that the proposal is an admirable one. It was felt that the only obstacle ,in the way of this economical and cominonsense arrangement was the fact that the ,Market is allowed to .keep open until a very late hour on Satur- day. As will he seen, however, from the in- terviews we have had with:a very consider- able number of the stallholders in the Market, they, too, see the absurdity of remaining at business when, by means of a general agree- ment, there. could be a beneficial curtailment of hours. It is certain that the tradesmen of the town and of the Market are in a mood to effect a reform :which will take very little effort, but will tell powerfully. in .the national interests in many ways. There' would be no flaring lights at night, and. Consequently no need for vexatious prosecutions. There would be a great saving of money as represented by the price of unnecessary gas and electricity which is now set going only, to, be obscured. But, most important of all, the long .and dis- graceful hours which girls and *Omen are now forced to .stand on Saturdays 'Would be short- ened, and life, for many assistants would be made tolerable.' The parties being agreeable, as We say; it remains only for the Markets Committee to take the matter in hand; for from our extensive inquiries, we are convinced that the Market hours are the. pint on, which the whole question turns. pr. public,, of course, have some responOility .in the matter. They can shop early, :as we are in- formed, many are doing.. . In this respect a . special appeal may be made to the' tract() unionists of the town. They clO, not like long unnatural hours of labour themselves; they can have a very big voice in saying thakethers shall not be unduly afflicted by night labour.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
GOSSIP.

Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart., was present at the banquet at the House of commons, on Thursday., in honour of Mr. Hughes, the Australian Prime Minister, who is on a visit to England.

"We thank the shareholders for their gener- osity—tempered by the thought that it is well deserved on our part," was Mr. Dwerryhouse'.c. concise reply when the annual meeting of Richmond's Gas Stove Co. voted/ him and his co-directors remuneration to the, 4tent of £B5O.

The shareholders laughed; they themselves had had 10 per cent. on their investments, so that there was all round satisfaction.

On Judge Scott Fox, K.C., taking his seat at the County Court on Thursday, he apologised for not having attended on the last Court day, explaining that he had been ill for a fortnight.

Mr. Arthur Browue expressed the pleasure of the solicitors at his recovery.

Before the Judge entered Mr. Browne caused a' surprise among • his fellow practitioners by announcing that he had been called to the Colout's and, to make his ease prima facie, pro- duced his military form. 'There itwas with the usual military preciseness and certainty: " Arthur Browne-, Oakville, Latchford Without, Class .3."

After gaining a dramatio effect, and his friends having asked • him "What are you going to do about it?" (in manner quite concerned) he explained that he had replied stating that, un- tortunately, he was long past military age and, therefore, some mistake must have been made.

Really, I suppose, the authorities were aiming at Mr. Browne's son, but that gentleman had well anticipated them by joining the Army some time ago. Another case of "Too late." (Lloyd George)

The Judge at County Court had his charm- ing wife and son as his 'companions in his judicial alcove. .T4is bright, golden haired boy of about seven or eight years sat between his parents with becoming dignity, and never spoke a word for hours, though 'occasionally be threw wondering glances at his mother.

When some plans wiu.e handed up to his Honour. the boy leaned forward and eyed them with tha interest of an assessor—or of someone who had not/seen such puzzling diagrams be- fore. I wager he cross-examined his father about them on the way home.

Mr. W. Madden, a barrister well-known on Warrington platforms, was in the main ease, and the air was full of witticisms.

The action had reference to an accident during a. gale, and a witness mentioned that owing to the plaintiff having been rendered hors de Coin- bat he had gained some of his customers..

"It's an ill wind that blows no one any good," commented Mr. Wingate Saul, barrister, and Mr. Madden, on the opposing side, promptly added, "It's blown us here!"

It had. Also nine prominent Warrington tradesmen as a jury. A pleasant Way of spend- ing their half-holiday!

At the Town Council meeting on Tuesday, Councillor Henshall raised a point of_order, and having vented • more than the customary sentence or two, the Mayor pulled him up by saying, "You 'are trying to make a speech!' "No, your Worship," replied the ',Councillor. "I'm making one without trying."
