MARCH 1916.

RICHMOND'S DIVIDEND.

Ten Per Cent., A Bonus, And A Reserve Of £60,000.

LOYAL WORKMEN.

At the twenty-fifth annual meeting of the shareholders of the Richmond Gas .Stove and Meter Co., Ltd., held at Warrington on Thurs- day, Mr. Thomas Dwerryhouse presided over an attendance including Mr. IT. M. Thornton .(vice- chairman and managing director), Mr. J. A. Ransome (managing director), Mr. J. Coson, Dr. J. 'F. Atkins, Mr. J. W. Glover and Mr. 'Jas. W. Slack, directors; and Messrs. F. A. ,Erost, J. Smethurst, J. Plant, S. Owen. A. Ben- nett, J. L. (*rice,' L. A. Voisey (auditor) and Arthur Slack (assi,:tant secretary).

===== 0002601_19160311_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
£2,400 FOR SOLDIERS' DEPENDENTS.

The directors' report, in .addition to dealing with the figures mentioned by the Chairman in his speech, recorded the. fact that the directors have continued the allowances to dependents of the Men who have joined his Majesty's Forces, the amount contributed during the year -being £2,422 Gs. 641. The .re-election of the retiring directors, Mr. John A. Itansoine, Dr. John F. Atkins and Mr. Jas. W. Slack was reported in accordance with the Company's Articles of Association. .

===== 0002601_19160311_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
A VERY SATISFACTORY REPORT.

The Chairman, in moving the adoption of the report and balance sheet, said he thought they would come to the conclusion that it was a,very satisfactory one, •(Hear, hear.) The year's business had been extremely trying owing to the continual changes in prices of materials, the scarcity of ,workmen, and the unrest among the latter as regards wages. Despite all drawbacks, the directors were able to submit a report for the past twelve months which would compare favourably with those presented in previous years. (Hear, hear.) The works at Grappen- hall had been further extended by the addition of the enamelling shop and furnace department. The building of the offices, in a general way, would complete the works at Grappenhall, and the management of the entire business at one place would save the company considerable ex- pense. Advantage had been taken of an oppor tunity of disposing of that portion of the works then vacant on the west side of Academy-street. The property of the company had been insured against air raid risks. The amount spent on new buildings was less this year than last by about £6,400; and they would notice with pleasure that the item " for goodwill" was gone. (Hear, hear.) He called that a blessing. (Laughter.) Stock-in-trade was up considerably, owing mainly to a heavier cla4s of business and a larger stock of iran. Invest- ments they had placed at their value at the end of the year. Last year they did not depreciate any because of the uncertainty of their value, consequent_upon the war. The 44 per cent. War Stock, investment of £4,550 IRB was made up of £1.862 18s. 2.1.- per cent. Consols, depreciated to £1.550 18s., supplemented by a cash payment of 0,000.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
The Disposable Balance;

The final result of the year's working, con- cluded the Chairman, is a disposable balance of £38,111 is., which the directors recommend ba appropriated as follows ;.----Depreciation on laiidings, plant, patterns, fixtures, &c., £7,0515 .a.t.; civieend on preference shares at the -rate of 6 per cent, per annum (less income tax), £5,412 2s. Id.; dividend on ordinary shares at the rate of 10 per cent. per annum (free of in- come tax), £8,161 -125.; bonus of 6d. per share on the ordinary shares, £2,040. Bs.; reserve fund .(bringing this amount up to £60,000), £5,000; balance to next account, subject to directors' 'remuneration. £10,401 Bs. Bd. A reserve fund of £60,000 was a very respectable amount in a concern with issued Capital amounting to £185,000. (llear, hear.) Altogether it was a balance sheet of which the shareholders of the company might feel proud. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160311_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
GAS AND PATRIOTISM.

. Mr. Thointon, seconding, said the operations, of the year, with its: resultant financial success, had further strengthened the company's already strong position. It was not. sufficiently known .that it was from the manufacture of coal gas that many indispensable constituents required by the country to-slay was largely obtained,. and it was. therefore a national necessity that coal gas should he used in increaSing quantity. For that reason alone it was cleac that the manu- factures of the firm were of the greatest import- ance-to the country, for without the millions of stoves in. use, the present great consumption of gas would not be taking place. By consuming gas for every purpose they were also helping forward that economy in expenditure so strongly recommended, for with ;the present price of coal and the difficulties of getting it, gas was un- doubtedly cheaper to use for most purposes. They, *ere fortunate to lay the foundations of the gas furnace department long before the war commenced, and by the gradual building up of entirely new and economical types of furnaces. the3r,were ready for the great demand that had since been experienced. Another department that had strained their resources of output was that of large cooking apparatus for hospitals, canteens, -industrial works, &c. The help of the firm in this direction had proved of value in the provision of apparatus exactly adapted for the varying requirements of special cases. He was satisfied that when peace arrived there would be even greater opportunities for develop- ment of gas supply than had been hitherto ex- perienced, and, as in the past, the firm would have their full share of the trade being done. (Applause.) The resolution was carried.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD DIVIDEND AND RESERVE.

The Chairman moved: "That a dividend at the rate of 10 Per cent. per annum (less 5 per cent. already paid) and a bonus of 6cl. per share on the ordinary shares, for the year ended December 31st, 1915, free of income tax, be de- clared and paid." • • • Arr. Coxon seconded, and said the dividend to be declared, in spite of the uncertainties of the past year. was very satisfactory. (Hear, hear.) Besides this, as no one could Isee what was in store, the directors felt they ought to add materially to the reserve fund and provide a good carry-for Ward. , Many of their men had joined the colours. Mr. Frank Richmond, son of the founder of the firm, and Mr. S. P. Thorn- ton, brother of their managing director, held commissions, and they felt proud of them. (Ap- plause.) DIRECTORS' REMUNERATION.

Alderman Smethurst proposed that £850,. free of income tax, be placed at the disposal of the directors for their services during the year. In doing so he congratulated the directors -upon the satisfactory result of their year's work, and said he was glad to see they had not forgotten the dependents of the. workmen who were serv- ing in the .war, an allowance having been made in the balance sheet for them Of • a sum of .t 2,422. .(ilear',. hear.) Mr. John Plant seconded, and it was carried.

Drowned in Sankey Brook.

Found drowned in Sankey Biook on Saturday, the body of an unknown man was the subject of an inquest at the Police Station on Tuesday morning, and the jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned." The man was Mt. sin. in height, of medium build, and wore a dark coat and vest and •brown corduroy trousers. 'Appar- ently he was of about 40 years of age, and had been in the water for some time. nesday.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Alderman Bennett v. Councillor Roberts.

The Museum Committee having sent .a petition to the Prime Minister againSt the closing of the, Museums in London, Alderman Bennett,. at the Town Council meeting on Tuesday, declared the Government's action to be a stupid and, ruble measure of . economy; and. Councillor Henry Roberts replied that the recommendation of the Committee of 'Retrenchment on this matter ought never to have been opposed. Economies had to be made at this time, and the closing of museums in London was one of the economies which could be effected with the least possible loss of value to the community at large. Special provision had been made in the recommendation of the committee, so that no student should be denied reasonable and proper access to the specimens and books relating to any .particular study.—The minutes were approved. Liberal Club for Wounded Soldiers.

On the initiative of Alderman Tinnion it has been decided to place the Liberal. Club at the disposal of wounded soldiers; and the following letter has been sent to the local military hospitals by Mr. Herbert Johnson, the hon. secretary : •

Liberal club, :Bewsey-street. 9th March, 1916. Dear Sir,—At a meeting of the committee held last night, the question of making some pro- vision for the entertainment of wounded soldiers was considered, and, it was decided to throw open the club to them from the hours of 2.30 to 6 p.m., and to provide them with billiards at id. for 50 and 2d. per 100, with tea at id. per cup, and minerals at Id. per bottle. The use of the bowling green, when opened, at Id.- per head. There' is at this club, excellent accom- modation, the bowling green is a large one, there are five billiard tables and a,geod .reading room. The committee hope that these facilities will be taken advantage of.

Death of Mrs. Elizabeth Brookes, Darlington Lodge. By the death of Mrs. • Elizabeth Brookes, at the age of 84, Warrington loses another of its old and much honoured residents. Mrs. Brookes was the widow of Mr. William Brookes, of Darlington Lodge, and pasSed, away at her old home. She had been in frail health for some months, and passed to rest on Saturday after- noon. Mrs. Brookes enjoyed in a peculiar degree the respect and affection of her friends, and will be much missed by the congregation of the Parish Church, in the services of which she had a deep and constant interest.

The funeral took place on Tuesday at the Warrington Cemetery, the officiating clergy being the Rev. Canon Willis and the Rev. J. M. Buckmaster. Among the mourners present tlt the funeral were Mr. William Brookes, Mr Samuel Brookes. Mr. John Brookes (sons), Miss Brookes, Miss Marion Brookes (daughters), Mr J. Proud, Mr. W. A. Proud (nephews), Mrs. Davenport (niece), Miss E. Unsworth (niece), Mr. and Mrs. Harry Hindley, Mr. Kenneth Brookes (grandson). Mr. Fred Bunch (great nephew). Mr. Robert Cuppage (nephew), Mr. E. Coates, Mr. Kirkland, Mr. Henry Taylor, and Mr. John Pharaoh, Messrs. J. Mounfield and Sons carried out the funeral arrangements.

Name Of Cromwell Recalled by Fire-. After lighting the boiler fire in order to com- mence business at the chip-potato • shop of Mr. Looker, ,92, C4ureh-street, on Wednesday even- ing, an assistant had occasion to leave for a short time, and on returning she found the shop to be on fire. Information was im- mediately conveyed to he Warrington rire Station. The first brigade, with the motor engine, Were soon on the scene, and three powerful jets soon • extinguished the flames. Much Of the 'shop's stock was damaged by fire and water, and the loss is not insured. The scene of the outbreak is one of the three picturesoue: buildings which date back to the time of Cromwell.

Good Humour as Asset of Church Life. The Rev. W. Clulow, of Lowton, paid a riiit to the Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church on 'Thursday, preaching in the afternoon and delivering a. lecture on "Goodness and Good il-Amour" in the evening, wheri Mr. George Weaver took the chair.

Goodness and good humour, said the Lec- turer, always went together. Good humour was essentially kindly • and. sympathetic, and Could not be unkind or .cause pain and suffer- ing. We needed good humour as well as good- ness, and should not be afraid of happiness There were people who thought that all mirth was madness. Martin Luther believed in the gospel of good humour, and nothing stood him in better stead than that belief. John Wesley ,was noted for his playful humour and always had a sunny face and cheerful disposition. We should all try to possess the same spirit of kindliness as he. We needed this healing sense' of humour in our church life. Those who manufactured trouble did so because they lacked the sense of humour. It was the want of (rood humour which made people so ready to take offence, and incapable of forgiveness. Even a religious sermon had, at times, a touch of humour in it, and during these dark,' dreary, war (Jays we needed the assistance which good linmour would never fail to give.

MARKET STALLHOLDERS'

POSITION.

We have made extensive inquiries of .the tradesmen of the town and learn that there is a general, agreement that there should be earlier closing of shops in town; and that provid- ing the Markets Committee would close the Market earlier the reform would be rapidly Pff acted. As will -be seen from our interview of the Market stallholders there is strong feel- ing among them favourable to: early closing.

WILL THE M4YOR CALL TRADESMEN TOGETHER?

Sir,—Thank you for your timely ankle on the above in to-day's " Examiner." I think you are right in stating the late hours of tho Markets as the cause of late hours in shops. - Perhaps some day when the war is over, and the shopkeeper is aroused to a new activity by the business problems confronting him, he will inquire whether the Markets were originally,in- tended for some of the businesses carried on there, and whether the Corporation ate justified in letting some of the pace for the purpose it iS new put to, not only creating an unfair competition with people who pay heavy rents And rates, but keeping shopkeepers from closing at a reasonable time,. aml preventing assistants from enjoying reasonable leisure. I feel sure if our worthy Mayor called a meet- ing of shopkeepers in the main streets for the Purpose of discussing earlier closing, there would be a niajerity in favour of closing earlier in normal times; and much earlier while the darkening Order is in force,. provided the Markets do the same. :This would -save .us from the harassing fear of a visit from the police to point out some unsuspected ray of light, and elieve the police of: a duty which I must say they have carried out in a patient and ex- tremely courteous manner.

BRIDGE-STREET.

MARKET STALLHOLDERS'.

XX ould Welcome Earlier Closing

Regulations. An "Examiner" representative interviewed' a number of stallholders in the Market on Wednesday and found the majority were pre- pared to work in harmony with their confreres of the shops. The first one called upon was Councillor F. Lindley, wholesale fruit merebant:, wins said). - • "As far as am- concerned, the PrepoSed earlier closing of the Market would not affect my business We are always closed before dark. I do not see why all of us cannot close before lighting-up time, for, after all, the public must buy necessities, and if they realise that estab- lishments close, at a stipulated time there would be no inconvenience caused." Too Dark To Give Change.

'Ain F. Bailey. fruiterer, said "I for one would welcome earlier closing, for the 'present conditions are totally Unsatis- factory. For some time I have only had one lamp lit over my stall and have been warned by the authorities that even then the light was too powerful.' The nuisance at present is that 'one cannot see to give change, for on each customer tendering silver or notes one has to iourney under the light to give the balance. There is no reason, as far as. I can see, why the public should not shop earlier, and I for one agree to do the same as the shopkeepers." The Question of Packing Up.

- Councillor James nalliwell, draper, boot and shoe dealer, was not quite so optimistic, for he said that he thought an earlier closing of the 'Market was not quite practicable. "We have not the same advantages .as the shops," said he, "for they are in the happy position of being able to close their shutters in a few minutes. We must have light. It is only we stallholders who know the trouble of Packing up after sales have been completed. It takes fully an hour or an hour and a half to perform this task, and it means even if we do fall in line with the shopkeepers that we shall have to give up selling before our more fortu- nate brethren."

Ladies Are Shopping Earlier. Mr. T. Spencer, ladies' and gentlemen's out- fitter, said, on being asked his views:— " To be candid, the majority of my customers .are ladies and already I am • finding that they are shopping several hours earlier. This is a -good .sign and one which I hope will be carried out by all purchasers. Trade is as brisk as ever, maybe a little better, for to my mind I 'think that the public' do not care to be about dn. the darkened streets."' , in Opposition.

Mr. James West, hosier, a stallLolder in the IS Market, for forty years, sounded 41. note of opposition "I call it abominable," said he ," that one 'should be called upon on such a matter. We have done all in, our power to comply with the regulations of those in authoritative quarters. We, at any rate, ought to be allowed to keep ()Peg until 10 p.m. at the earliest. Trade is good, I admit, but, I think we have already made many sacrifices. The lighting regulations are being complied with quite in keeping with The instructions, and therefore I do not see the Ilecessity for closing the Market and interfering :with our trade." Early Closing A Boon To All. .

SAME AS UST YEAR.

Town Council Approve Levying

Of 9s. 4d. Rate.

ECONOMIES FAIL TO LOWER

LOCAL TAXES.

Despite the strictest attention to economy, the Warrington Town 6nricil have consid- ered it expedient to fix a rate of 9s. 4d. in the X., the same as last year. ,

The Council sat on Tuesday, and after a lucid statement by the ."Chancellor of the Exchequer," Councillor John Wilkinson, and a lamentation from Alderman Bennett, un- animously agreed to the three impositions proposed—Poor Bate, 55.; Improvement Rate, 45.; and General Sewers Rate, 4d. ' To solace ratepayers it Was emphasised that economies had been effected wherever possible.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
STATIONARY RATES IN EXTRAORDINARY

TI NIES.

Councillor WILE :N ;UN, introducing , the Budget, said:—.

_ . Considering the extraordinary times through which we are. passing and the increased cost of almost every commodity, it will be. souie satis- faction to the Council and the ratepayers to know that for the coming year at least,.there is to be no increase in the rates. Not only will the total of 9s. 4d. in the t be unchanged. but it is proposed to levy them exactly as last year.. Poor Rate 55., Improvement Rate 45., ani .43erieral Sewers Rate 4d. There have been many factors which have operated to make this pos- sible. and I will, very briefly, indicate some of the most interesting Of them. Details of Rates.

On die Borough Rate the Watch Committee's requirements will be legs by .tl,OOO, due mainly to the reduction of .1800 in the wages, some 35 members of the Force having joined the Army, and the work is being carried on by a very much. reduced staff. _

_ The Town Hall, Baths, Weights and Measures, Bridges, Health and Parr Hall estimates are all somewhat lower; but there is a slight increase in . the Cemetery Committee and the .1-Inane° Committee ask for £5OO more. The reason for this is principally that the call for the lunatics 'is estimated at £7OO more, and loan charges at £270 more, whilst printing ,and stationery show a reduction of 1200, as does the Exchequer con- tribution • account. As it is anticipated: there will be no elections this rear, £lBO less will be required on this account. One further 'item in this estimate calls for a remark; £450 is given as the likely deficiency on the motor 'buses. a full year instalment of the loan having. to be met, and this loss has. to be made a. direct charge upon the rates. The education .call is again to be £29,000, this, as you know, being equal to a rate of 2s. in the I.'. The summary of the estimates on the Borough Rate shows a total requirement .of £49,906, a sum which will be levied- by precept upon the overseers, to be added by them to then— own expenditure and to- that of the Guardians of the Poor to form the Poor Rate. In the Improvement Rate much the same kind of" give and take" will be noticed. The Sani- tary Works Departments require £tB2 more, due Mainly to the greatly increased cost of hay, straw and provender in the Cleansing Depart- ment. and the increased cost of fuel in the Long- ford Department. The Health and Hospitals Departments taken together show £302 less, the Highway Department shows £1,3.53 less, and the Scavenging Department 060 less. In the former case -£420 is due to wages and £742 for paving •materials and cartage; in the latter case it is • due mainly to wages. The Health Com- mittee are suggesting the .purchase of a small two-seater car, estimated to cost £2lO, for the use of the Medical Officer of Health. The need.. for a. quicker method of transit for hini is very. apparent. ,as it will .enable him to devote much more time to his medical duties at Sankey, and. silo save much valuable time: in moving about the.town. The Fire Engine, Parks, Markets and: Allotments .a re each rktuiring- somewhlet The Street Inmirovethent • and. •Lighting • Com-. mittee's:estimate is-greatly reduced, being £3,030 below last year:due, as will be quite apparent. • to the very extensive lighting restrictions which: are at present operative within this borough, as elsewhere, and which will no doubt continue. until the conclusion of the war.. As against this reduction we must sot the inerease in the esti-, mate of the .Finance Committee, where loan charges call for, in round figures. £l,OOO 'more, and another £l,OOO has been added to cover air- craft. insurance (unfortunately considered neces- sary), possible extra taxation and other con• tingencies. The total estimate shows that £52.320 will be required, a small draw of £57 on the balance being made, to make the figure agree with the exact estimated product of a 4s. rate., The General Sewers Rate is again to be levied at. fourpenee. which will, however, not be sufficient by some £5OO, but this will be Partly met out of a possible balance in hand, and the remainder will be carried forward to next year. In the near future, though possibly not next year, it tuay be found that a fourpenny rate will not be sufficient for Sewers Rate purposes. In this estimate appears for the first time an item for Poor • Rate, it having been decided by a House of Lords judgment that sewers are rate- able.

Trading Aids for Rates.

The contributions f min the profit-earning undertakings in aid of the rates total 4:7,000, being £l,OOO from the Gas, 5:2,090 from the Tram- ways and £l,OOO from the Electricity. These are the same figures as last year, but are very. much below the average of many years previous. It may, in the near future, be necessary for the Council to consider whether the transfer from these departments could not be consider- ably increased.

Savings and Increases Upon Last Ycar's Estimates.

Just a word here may be desirable on the working , out of the estimates of the year now drawing to a close. On the Borough Rate there have been -savings on Many items, such-as print- ing and stationery, registration of voters, election expenses, •cost 'of prosecutions, and for police wages, clothing and other items, but taxes, rates and fuel have been increased, and there has been unfortunately an increased call of 1:625 by the Lancashire Asylums 'Board for lunatics. It is expected that the net result will be a saving, and the working balance on this rate being thereby increased somewhat le- yond. our requirements, will enable us to take X 1,093 out of it and so reduce the call upon the overseers from £51,034 to £.19,900, without which reduction it would have been found im- possible to levy the same Poor' Rate as 'before, as the figure to be included in that rate for "Union purposes is somewhat More than was esti- mated a year ago. In, the Improvement .Rate there have been suvings on many items, notably. on the Tuber- culosis Dispensary, which has not been working, as it has not been found possible to obtain the services • of a suitable medical • man.; on the SankeV Sanatorium, where the proposed extcn- sionS have not been carried. out; in the High- way Department for road materials andeartage ; in the Markets Department • for the suggested fish stalls and butchers' stalls when the Market Hall should be pulled down; in the Cleansing Department for materials for pails and ashtubs, and for horses and other itenis; and in the light- ing charges for the borough through the partifl! restrictions which have been operative over 'a considerable portion of the year. • Against theso. savings we must, however, set the cost of the new Pavilion at the hospital, the erection of which and .the fitting up has cost £1,456 up to date, to be paid for entirely Out of revenue; in- creased rates and .taxes; dearer provender, hay and straw; great increase in the cost of fuel for Longford Depot, and, for a proposed motor lorry for the Highway Department. The net result will' probably be a small saving, which will leave the working balance slightly better than it was expected to be, but it is yet far from being sufficient properly to tide over the first few months of the financial year. I think this summary.will give-youn good idea of the many changes which have had to be taken into aecount., when compiling the various esti- mates of the.. committees and of .the process • by which the final figures are reached, and when, yOu have Studied the full details in the published Budget yeti' will, I am, sure,. be •satisfied that the committees, 'generally, .have endeavoured to keep within reasonable limits and have exit down their expenditure as far as is consistent with,

the requirements of the borough under the pre- sent abnormal conditions, the increased price of almost everything, rendering it impossible to do more in this direction than they have done.:

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 11. 1916.

Patriotism of Local I nvestors.

As Chairman of the Finance Committee I should like to say a word about the question of the loans. The changes in the money market due directly to the war, and to the issne by the Government of the War Loan and Exchequer Bonds have necessitated considerable changes in the loan dAt of this Corporation. The best efforts of mt.committee, aided by, the Borough Treasurer, have been put forward to meet the new conditions, and it is with con- siderable pride that - I am able to say, that, taken as a. whole, our investors have stood loyally' by us. (Hear, hear.) , It is really in- teresting to note that in spite of somewhat higher offerg elsewhere, the majority of our clients remain faithful to the good Old town. One particularly interesting feature has been the way in which several of our local firms have been willing to lend to us considerable sums, a publie-spirited Ahich is .eXtreinely com, mendable,at the, present time. These firms, in. spired‘bf a trne patriotic feeling, have not ova' looked the'grenter 'claims of the nation, butlave been to' spare some portion of their ,re- serves for us. The Borough Treasurer has been pleased to report 'to the committee that War- rington:firms are pioneers in this direction, and that many other towns have, since followed suit (Hear, hear.). I feel sure there are still other firms in the town • who will, if occasion should require, help our local exchequer on similar lines. A few thousands Obtained in this way, here and there, would be a substantial assistance to us, in tiding over the occasional awkward position, caused by the withdrawal of money from our. funds. -

I cannot conclude without saying how sorry we all were, to hear of the illness Of the Borough .Treasurer—(hear, hear)----who has had, of late, a very anxious time. I: am pleased to hear of his improving health, and we shall all be de- lighted to have him again among us, when fully restored. (Ilear, hear.). On behalf of my corn. raittee I should- also like to express our high appreciation of the ability and zeal of Mr. P:iir- hurst and Mr.- Woodcock, and the loyal support they receive from the members of their respec- tive staffs. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160311_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
NO EXTRAVAGANCES.

Councillor LOCKER .haring seconded, Alderman BENNETT expressed his disappointment that the rates were to be the same as those of last year. They had been accused in previous years of con- siderable extravagance, and the opinion of the town appeared to have been that that.extrava- gance, had been reflected in the very high rates. This year, however, no one could accuse them of extravagance, yet the rates had to remain at their • present high figure, and there did not seem any immediate prospect of a. reduction. Having declared that educational efficiency must be maintained, even if it involved a heavy cost, Alderman Bennett spoke of. the patriotism of those local people who, .in the matter of loans, had stuck- to their own town, even when more advantageous offers were made from 'other sources. They must ,not, however, count upon a continuance of that support. He was very much afraid that it might prove ephemeral, and that they might have to face considerably higher rates of interest in the future. 'The Alderman also regretted the slow productivity of the borough rate. It was only growing at a snail's pace, and he thought the,reasons were the prac- tical cessation of building of houses for the working classes and • the comparative failure of the efforts of the Corporation to attract new industries to the town. '

===== 0002601_19160311_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGHER RENTS.

Thousands Of Houses Affected En Warrington.

RATEABLE VALUE UP BY £3,688.

• -• -During •tllO past: ye 4-. there. has been .:an increase of a few thousand pounds in the rate- able value oi. the borough, and also wholesale alterations in ,the rents of houses, due mainly to the higher rates placed upon us twelve months . ago. , • .These facts were • told by Mr. Arthur Bottoinley to a meeting .Of the Assessment Committee on Wednesday afternoon, • when he reported on the new valuation list. lie said that the net increase over that of last year was :t3,688, after allowing for reductions by the Assessment Committee for demolished property and for assessments reduced. At the present rates in the 4 this wouhr produce about £1.500 per annum, equal to nearly a 11d. rate; The work had been very considerable, about 14,000 rentals having been ascertained: The compila- tion of the list had been carried out by the Overseers' Department during the last four months..

===== 0002601_19160311_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
2.500 Lucky Tenants.

Of thq ascertained rents \it was found that only about 2,500 had been unaltered during the last twelve months. About 4,000 had been in- creased so much as to cause an increase in the assessment in spite of the operation of the lower scale, and about 7,500 had been altered only sufficiently to counter-balance the scale. As there were about 10,000 alterations a year before, this showed how considerable had been the movements in rentals during the last two years, and but for the operation of the new Rents Act there seemed little doubt but that further increases would take place, ,for it was evident that the present figures did not reach the limit at which similar houses in other towns were rented.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
Property Owners Shelve Burden.

In fairness, however, it must be pointed out that the large .increase of the rates twelve months ngo from Bs. to 9s. 4d. was 'accountable for many alterations of rents during the last twelve months.

The figures of the new valuation list were :

Gross Value. 4398,390 10s. Rateable Value (Buildings), ~V 111,955. Rateable Value (Agriculture), £1,419 10s.

The excellent results fully justified the labour involved and the many changes showed how necessary it was that the work should be done.

ST. PAUL'S CONGREGATIONAL SOCIAL.

The COMillff Peace.

St. Paul's annual congregational Social took place on Tuesday in the Girls' School, Ken- drick-street, the programme being curtailed owing to the lighting restrictions. The whist drive and -dance were diSpensed with and only the concert retained: This, however, was of w higher standard, a two hours' -capital pro- gramme being provided for an audience of about 200, by the Brooklyn Quartet (Messrs. Seplitow and Dutton, and Mesdames tSilcock and Eckersley), Mr. R. IL Pearson (violinist); and I r. IL M. Ashton (humorist). The Rev. E. Downham, who occupied tile! chair, regretted the absence of the Vicar (the :Rev. W. Bracecamp, now on active service), and of Mr., J. It Smethurst (vicar's warden); I who was confined to his room with a severe cold. This was, 'he said, the second year that the annual social had been held under the cloud of war. Last year the hope was expressed that peace would have been heralded before another social was held. Such a hope last year was doomed not to be realised. There were now solid reasons why they might hope that before, another social came round victory, would have been attained and peace proclaimed. On had real assurances for this hope now in recent I happenings. ' Germany had been dealing sledge- hammer blows at Verdun and elsewhere and had not only been held, but had had inflicted upon'her forces crushing losses. The British had maintained their lines and more, and Russia , had done such things in the East as had made Turkey begin :to weary of the war, and Bulgaria to throw Out feelers for peace. At horns we had stood the demand for men, munitions and money: • All these. filets were. forerunners of a peace that`waidd be abiding. . On the motion .of' Ir. A: C. BinkS (people's warden), ,seCOnded by Mr. E. Wilkes (organist),l a vote of thanks was passed to the .artistes. _ _

Refreshments and time of gocial inter- course followed".

SHORTAGE OF HOUSES.

People May Have To Live !n Iron Buildings.

TOWN COUNCIL'S CONCERN

Alarmed at the shortage of houses in Warring- ton, Alderman James Evans, at the Town Coun- cil meeting on Tuesday, urged the necessity of consulting 'with other Corporations on the sub- ject, With a view to tackling the problem at the earliest opportunity. - Being Chairman of the Health Committee, the Alderman. naturally looked at the problem mainly from a hygienic standpoint and con- tended that the scarcity of houses 'would 'react. greatly upon the health Of; the inhabitants. - Although the resolution proposed was carried netn con, it gave rise to some piquant passages in the debate. The 'notion read follows:

, That 'in the opinion of this Council legisla- tion is necessary to simplify and 'cheapen the transfer of -land and, 'buildings, so as to encourage the building' of houses for the work- ing: clast;es; and that other Corporations-be re- quested to join in asking the Local government Board,' and through them Parliament, to receive a deputation upon the subjeet."

===== 0002601_19160311_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE ENTERPRISE LAGS.

Alderman EVANS declared that all over .thp country; but especially in Lancashire, the hous- ing question was becoming one of great import- ance, and would have to be-seriously considered by municipal bodies : They had only to observe what was taking place in Warrington to -realise the urgency of the position. In 1907 190 houses were. built, and the number of unoccupied houses was 418. In 1908, new houses built were 241, unoccupied 434-; 1909; new houses 237, Un- occupied 553; 1910, new houses 202, unoccupied 511; 1911, new houses 221, unoccupied 603; 1912, new houses 99, unoccupied 460; 1913, new houses 87, unoccupied 169; 1914, new houses 58, un- occupied 113; 1915, new. houses 15, unoccupied 70. The figures were ,eloquent of themselves. "The main object - I have in bringing the matter forward," continued the Alderman, "is the health of the people, because it is. in refer- ence to that subject that the Health committee is principally concerned. (Hear, hear.) The people are not being housed under favourabl?. conditions, owing . to .the scarcity of . houses, which means that two families will be found in one house, On -the bther hand we all know that as houses become .more scarce it is more than likely that rents will go up. A limited in- come can only be spent once a week, and if the cost of housing is to be'higher there will be less of something else, and • that will affect the stamina-and health of the. child-life of the town. Some course must be .adopted to. open up the building -trade, and .the usual supply of houses ,must be got, or that duty will come upon the shoulders of the. municipality-, who will have to adopt the Housing Act and build themselves. A better method is to try to -open out a way for private enterprise to step in and to supply exist- ing needs. I do not know of a case where, the Housing Act has been adopted where there has not been a serious-lOss to the ratepayers." If .all Corporations presented a -deputation to the Local Government Board, concluded the. Alderman, be thought it would have great effect. Personally, his opinion was. that municipalities should at least own all the freehold land within the borough which ,were likely .sites for them to erect dwellings on, and so become the first leasers of the land to those who finally erected the houses.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
1909 FINANCE ACT BLAMED.

•Iri•seconding, Alderman BENNETT said he had no doubt that in the early future, unless some- thing drastic were ihine,:a house famine would have'to be faced. People had not been-too wet housed in the past, and it looked as if we were not going to be housed, ;It all in the future un- less some solution was found for this very im- portant problem. He attributed the cessation in building to the operation of the Finance; Act of 1909.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
HOUSES UNFIT FOR CATTLE.

Councillor DALE remarked that in a national crisis like the present .the question of housirg must be looked upons:Secondary. Wliftl the people'fnight'be Called 4pon to do after t;:ne war it wasl imPos,ible to predict, but if. we Pi-,uf;i. so to speak, 'anticipate' the completion of the war by entering into. consultation with other municipalities and make ,provision iO7 111,IJ:t3 definite action when the war was over, they Would be going on properlines. He .91.44p0rte(1 the resolution mainlron hygienic grounds; and hoped they would avoid the political question. Looked at from the hygienic point of view the question became entirely nbn.political. Not only. was there overcrowding through scarcity. o feottage property in, Warrington, ,but there were houses not worthy of the name. (Hear, hear.)

Notwithstanding the • great • improvements made, there were houses in which • men, and more especially women and children, were spending a large percentage of their time that were unfit for cattle to live in. It was having a serious effect )on the health of the people and the infantile mortality rate.. - . • Councillor RENSITALL said it Was no use blink- jag the fact that the Finance Act of 1909 had been the cause of the cessation in the building of houses. Builders had been treated as some- thing approaching 'lunatics and prudes. "There is no doubt that slums are dangerous, and bring about physical and moral disease," declared the DEPUTY MAYOR, "but it does not detract one bit from the urgency of the position put forward with regard to some of the higher classes of property." Ile thought private enter- prise would be best adapted for the higher class of property, and that Municipalities should pro- vide the other houses, if private enterprise would not cater, even though it might be done at a loss, for, .after all, it was a • health. question. The Corporation had to provide pure water and look after the sanitary arrangements of the town on grounds of health, - and, therefore, if need be, they should erect houses for the people to live in decently and healthily.

PRICE OF BUILDING THE MAIN CAUSE. Councillor -WILKINSON was of opinion that the cost of transferring land was not so great as to affect the erection of houses., We should get houses ,built when all the essentials for their erection came down to .the -normal .prices. Then private enterprise would proceed as in the past. We should never have cheaper houses:if we were continually harping on the Finance Act, for that had only affected matters in an infinitesimal degree. 'Speaking forcefully, as usual, Councillor PEMBERTON declared, that it was the Finance Act which was the cause of the trouble. Ninety- eight per cent. of the houses in the past had been built by speculative builders, and they had not stopped 'work out of pique or just for fun. It was because they had been too hard hit. "We are hundreds of thousands of houses short," he declared, "and whatever the Government or the Corporations do, you can never make it up. I predict that for the next ten years you can- not. produce-, and never will produce, the number of ,houses wanted in this country. The reason is , you , won't have Aheworkpeciple here: You are too far behind. This is goig to be the most serious question after the war that this country has even seen, and before you have done it will be necessary to ,build. galVanised iron houses for the people to live in." •

WOUNDED.

LYNCE-(ORPORAI, HoWARI).

S‘N immer And Boycler ()untied On

Anniversary Of Enlistment.

Mrs. F. Howard, of 26, Grafton-street„ :off Lovely-lane, on Friday evening of last .week received the news that her husband—Lance- Corporal Fred Howard—had been wounded in action. He is attached to the loyal Engineers, and . . has been in the firing-line for several months. It is singular. to relate that it is exactly a year to the day from the time of his enlistment-to the day he was wounded. In a • letter to his wife, dated February 28th, • he .states;—

"1 .am writing to you from the Casualty Clearing Station. I- came here, last night. I have had my back. braised ;,with a whizz-bang,'

Wounded.

going in the trenches at ten o'clock yesterday morning. It is nothing serious; so you need not upset yourself about me, because I will be away from the trenches for a while, and I will get a bed and a good rest. - Don't. write until you hear .front me again, if you have .already written. , I have told W. Jones to keep any letters that come and then he can forward them on to me. It is just twelve months by the date and tithe since I enlisted, and I was promoted on January 27th, so the 27th is a lucky and an unlucky date for me." I • . Lance-Corporal Howard was, prior to enlist- ing, .a joiner in the employ of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield & Song, and is highly esteemed. At present he is an inmate of the Royal Infirmary, Central .Branch, Rebey.street, Manchester, where his wife, who visited him on Slinday last, found him both cheerful and in good spirits. The Lance-Corporal, in civilian life, was a noted swimmer, for in 1903 he • was attached to the Warrington Swimming Club when they won the championship shield. lie was also a member of Messrs. Crosfield's Works' Squadron and is a gold medal winner for the year 1910. Bowling also claimed the woUnded soldier's attention, for he was 'a member nf the Bank Quay Bowling Club, having won several minor -prizes. _ _ .

Lance-Corporal Howard is the son of the late Mr. H. Howard, who was some years ago killed whilst ,following his employment at Messrs. Greening's.

CORPORAL HALLIA 00D.

Tal\ien Prisoner By Bnigart IA bile Covering Relreal.

. . "I am glad to be able to say-that Corporal J. Hal'wood, who was reported missing, is now reported as a prisoner in Bulgaria," says the Rev Gt, Vale. Owen in the " Orford Parish Magazine." "It seems the affair.hapPened'iail Peeernber 9th, when thO Company' to which Corporal llallwood belonged was on patrol helping to cover the retreat of the transport. It was very dark and foggy and they lost their way among the many mountain, passes and suddenly found themselvt— surrounded by the Bulgars.. A certain number manageil to break away. The remainder were taken prisoners. When it. was known that. the Company was missing there were plenty who volunteered to go out and find them. They were not allowed to do so, and half an bour later this reffisal was justified, as the road by which they would have had to go was being heavily shelled by the enemy."

FATHER LANE FOX.

Roman Catholic Chaplain Dangerously Wounded.

We regret to ,tate that information has been received by Father Whittle, of St. Alban's R.C. Church, that one of his curates, Father Lane Pox.. has been dangeromdy wounded while serving as a chaplain at the Front. We are • Pleased- to add that yesterday's bulletin indi- cated that the reverend gentleman is progressing favourably. Father Lane Fox is. we understand, the second Roman Catholic Chaplain to have been wounded. He now lies in the Red Cross Hos- pital at Calais, where, of coarse, he is receiving every attention.

Young, and of a zealous, energetic and eon• siderate disposition, it was only natural that at the outbreak of war ha. should seek to find scope for any service which he could render. He

therefore, almost immediately presented himself for service as a chaplain, and went to the Front soon after being accepted. Here, ltehas been extremely popular 'amongst those to whom he has ministered, and, in fact. with all those with whom he has come in contact. Many. letters have been received, speaking of his kindliness and helpfulness towards our brave lads at the r!cnt lie has not hesitated to share the bur- ,!ctis mud ..I:!Lters of men of the front line, and on many o,r asions has spent several (lays in the trenches with them.. He has had many narrow escapes, and has many exciting stories to relate.

The reverend gentleman 'came to St. Alban's twelve months before .the lyar, he then having just, completed his college 'course, and, his parishioners will be gratified to know that al- though he is suffering for his zeal in the country's cause, he has been as, popular a chap- lain as he was •a curate in their midst.

GALLANT TERRITORIAL.

Private Delaney Rescues Apparently

Prowited Comrade:

Private Peter F. Delaney, son of Mr. Peter Delaney, 19, Museum-street, has just done a par- tieularly brave act, 'which 'resulted in 'saving the life Of a fellow member of the 211th South Lancashire Regiment, in training in the South. A number of !Territorials were enjoying them- selves in the baths, and when Private. Delaney was in his dressing box- ,he heard the cry that one of the party was drowning at the, bottom_ IA the bath. Though partly dressed, he im- mediately plunged into the water and brought to the. surface the man. who was in such an extremely critical condition that it took an hiiur's artificial respiration to restore him, to life. Private Delaney has received the thankp of the whole battalion for his prompt and sue- eessful act. He is an expert swimmer, and a member of the Warrington Swimming Club.

MARRIED MEN REASSURED

We are authoritatively informed that the Military authorities, the Advisory Com- mittee, and -thee Tribunal in Warrington have now dealt. with .nearly the.- whole of the eases of single men of military ago brought in under the Military Service Act, and not teMporarily reserved by starg or badges.. - , •

They are now investigating the cases of the single men of military age who were badge 4 or starred under the voluntary system. These badges or stars are no absolute guar- antee that single men bolding them will not be required to go into the Army. In cases 'where it can be shown that it is possible to replace a man in a reserved occupation by one past military age .or by female labour it will be done.

ESTATE AGENT'S CLAIM.

Employe Of Sir Gilbert Greenall Exempted Till June.

Men residing in Stockton Heath and Grap- penhall appeared before the Runcorn Rural Tributial at Dutton Workhouse on Friday, Mr. C. E. Litiaker, sen., presiding.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
ESTATE AGENT'S CLAIM.

An assistant estate agent employed by Sir Gilbert Greenell, and residing at Lower Walton, applied for total exemption. He was the only son of a widowed mother, and was the sole sup- port of his mother and sister, who# could not go out owing to the frail health of 'his mother. He had also financial commitments, such as life insurance, which could not be met if he had to go.

- A letter was read from Sir Gilbert's agent, saying that the applicant had charge of thes records, &c., of Sir Gilbert's pedigree pigs and shorthorns, and also kept the accounts of four farms, and paid the wages, amounting to £l.OO each week. All arrangements for the transit of pigs and cattle after sales were in his hands. He had no other assistant in the office who could undertake the work, which was not at all suit- able for women.

In reply to the Military representative, the applicant said he did not think he would have any recompense from the estate, as he had only. been there for about 15 months.

Exemption was granted until June Ist.

Farmers Concerned At Losing

Their Labourers.

Most of the cases presented to the r_Aymm_ Tribunal last night were of farmers from the village and surrounding district. _

Exemption until the 10th of April. Farmer Who Helped.

A farmer of Statham claimed total exemp- tion'on the ground that he was in a certified trade. He had 77 statute acres, had 26 cows and three horses, was married and had no sons. He rendered assistance in manual labour on the farm in conjunction with one teamsman and a casual. He had no other assistants and superintended the whole of the work himself. Asked how many acres he had -:Eder the plough, he replied: "Forty-four." Applicant stated he might as well give up everything if he was taken away. The decision of the Tribunal was exemption, conditional on his remaining as occupier on a farm.

'HIE CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTOR.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Jim Bounce (3) stated • that he had a con- scientious objection to 'fighting. Ile didn't like the Germans, but recognised that they wete his spiritual brothers.—Vide "Punch." Sir,—The reports in the Press of the majority of the appeals made by "conscientious objectors" and their .replies to the questions addressed to them by members of the Tribunals furnish pitiful reading. That there are a few—very few—men in. this country who are really and truly opposed to war under any circumstance; who have otways consistently held this view, and who are. pre- pared to suffer for their adherence to their con- victions, I will not for a 'moment deny. I do say, however, most emphatically, that there are an infinitely greater number of men (?) who are not ashamed to masquerade in the "conscien- tious objector's" cloak in the hope that it may hide the contemptible meanness of their conduct and prevent the discovery of the rank selfishness of -their miserable souls.

Case after case ha&disclosed the damning fa 4 that, while the applicant for exemption has pleaded the strength of his "conscience" as an excuse for his objection to Personal combatant service,,heis at the same time employed in. the manufacture Of munitions of war, receiving moreAhan his. normal earnings in consequence, and therefore, so 'far as he is able, profiting to the full froni the ghastly shedding of blood which this war has compelled. • Safe in.the protection 'ensured for him by -the self-sacrifice of. his • fellow countrymen, he has the shameless .audacity to flaunt his so-called "religious convictions" before the world, alleg- ing-7-in some instances—that even if the Ger- mans got here and his mother was murdered or his sister violated in his•presence, he would not lift his hand against the perpetrators of such foul crimes!

Can such. men be sane? Surely .these- .facts disclose 'symptoms which suggest the necessity of a searching inquiry into the mental condi- tion of the applicants, and .when—as would be foulid in some instances—cerebral disease existed the individuals so affected shoukl be treated like other lunatics and duly interned. To the others, Who, while benefiting by the "blood money" which they are earning in munition works, and in regard to which their highly Sensitive con- sciences never raise the slightest sign of a pm- test; Who, judged out of their own mouths, stand condemned .as the most contemptible, Of hypocrites, haVing in mind the terrible issues which are at stake in this present Titanio struggle, and remembering that "lie who is not for us is against us," I 'would .apply the prin- ciple underlying the saying that "ho who will not work, neither shall he eat," and I would .say,_ . . ".,`..lf;your country not worth fighting :for, it is not:.woitli-,licing- in—get you •gone to some place where parasites and cowards are welcome, and make .room for honester men."

PATRIOT,

THE "INNOCENTS ' ABROAD.

That goes without say- ing, but when they come here they must register within forty days. In the majority of cases Irishmen were not registered. If the police attempted to do their duty.on the matter of registration the men would go back.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
ONE TAKEN, THE OTHER LEFT.

A Rixton farmer applied for the exemption of his two sons, whom he regarded as indispensable. Their occupation was one which was for the benefit of the State, and if his sons went away his financial position would be ruined. The Chairman (Mr. G. Mather) said that in reading the speeches of public men, he saw it was stated that it could not be understood why farmers employed their sons or relations. • The reason was that they could not get anyone else to do the work, especially if Sunday work had to be :undertaken. Nowadays men liked to have Saturday afternoon and Sunday to themselves, with the result they would not take on farming. Mr. W. Furness thought that one of the two ought to go and the farmer ought to obtain a substitute. _ _ _

The Chairman: I don't think the farmer can replace them. If labour was unobtainable the farmer would not be able to produce the same amount of reoi this year, and in after re.Ars it would be worse. Mr. J. Backhouse pointed out that in Essex the military representative had laid it down that it was essential for three men to attend to 100 acres. In that district they had adopted the principle of one man for 100 acres, but it de- pended on the class of farming. The public must be made to understand that the food sup- ply was going to s.uffer if they took men from the farms. If men were taken away the system of farming would be altered so as to conform with the labour available on the farm. _

One was -exempted for two months find the other 'provisionally.

Death of Former Lythm Resident.—We regret to record the death of Mrs. 3. Ilenshall, at her residence, Cadmant Park, Conway, which took place with rather painful suddenness oii*Tues- day morning. The news of her death was first conveyed bY telegram4o,Mr. W. E. Hillier, of tho Cross, Lymm..,,The late Mrs. Henshall was the: wife of Mr., J. Henshall, a well-known chemist, who, for upwards of 40 years was the sub-postmaster of Lymm. She was highly' respected, and her loss will be very keenly felt by the whole of the ,!‘rillage. The interment takes place td--day,ut the Lymm Parish Church.

Gunner W. Harrison Wounded By German Sniper.—Miss Amy :Barnes. ,of Newfields, yesterday received news that -Gr. W. Harrisrui. attached•to the ILE:A., is lying:in a yßouen hos- pital in consequence of limy:being wormdcsi by a German sniper.. Gunner Harrison resides High-lane, Lyzoni.,,and his age is 43 years. For some considerable time prior .to the war he was employed as a carter under Mr. W. Allman, of Whitbarrow-road, Lymm, but upon the out- break of war thought it his duty to assist his King and country. As a youth he was educated at the Lymm School, and worshipped at the Parish Church. Up to the present no further Iparticulars are available.

Volunteers at Dunham Massey.—An interest- ing sham-fight took place on Saturday at Dun- ham Massey between the Altrincham Battalion of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment, and the 6th Battalion Manchester Volunteers. The out- line of the 'scheme was that the Manchesters had to cut the railway line at Dunham Station, and the Altrincham Section were to prevent it. The rules were: (a) Any party outnumbered two to one were to retire; (b) 100 yards to be an effective range; (c) no operation to take place outside radius of two miles from Dunham Station; (d) operations not to commence b....fc,re 1.30 p.m. and "cease firing" to be sounded, by massed bugles, at 4.34) p.m. Great excitement prevailed in the early part of the afternoon, when the Manchesters showed great activity on their left flank. The Oughtrington and Lymm Platoons were on several occasions forced to retire, but at a critical moment the Bowdon Section reinforced them with a Maxim gun, and the invaders were eventually driven back. Not to be outwitted, the Manchesters made many attempts' to regain their former advantages, but it was unavailable. The decision was given that the Lymm Platoon were victors.

United Free Church Services.—The annual united service of the Free Churches was held in the Chem.-4ane Independent Methodist Chapel. The preacher wgs Mr. A. Monti&ld, of Warring- ton, and the sermon was one that bore reference to the unsettlement and distresses of the present day. Paul's reference to "The things which have happened to me" served as a text, and the -sermon dealt with the chequered side of the Apostle's career, of the well-intentioned visit to Jerusalem that put him in the hands of his enemies ,and made him. a Retrial' prisoner for four years, with, the consequent break-up of his missionary plans. Paul the missionary became Paul the prisoner, but with unbroken Zeal he proceeded to preach to the Roman soldiers who guarded him, and so spread Gospel , knowledge among Nero's bodyguard. In an interesting passage Mr. Mountield traced the introduction of the Gospel into Britain to the Roman sol- diers, and following that the labours of Patrick, who was grandson to one of the Romans, and through whom Ireland was evangelised. Paul's own programme failed, but a larger and better one resulted. Roman soldiers became mission- aries, who carried the Gospel to remote parts; the Apostle's prison letters reflecting his deep thinking were written, and have become the permanent treasures of the Church. Out of Paul's broken programme a very much better programme was evolved, one for which the Church and the world would be grateful to the end of time, and the fact that a kindly Provi- dence so arranged it must be their solace in the troubled days of upheaval through which they were passing. -

vision.' Without inspiration or vision the best work cannot be done, for, lacking it, duty must seem always dull and irksome. 'Where there isno vision 'the people perish,' for there is some- thing harsh and, repellant about •duty done against the grain, without the inspiration of something to uplift it and ennoble it; but when it is dope, not :only for its ow,,n sake, but desired for its own Sake, because it is to us the will of God, then, and then only, we begin to discern and enter into the enjoyment of what has been deSeribed aathe beauty of holiness." It is not the Rector continues, "so much what a man does as *bat he strives to do that makes him what he is. It is this, beauty of holiness' that flould be the desire and aim of all." •

The 'Rector concludes with :the, lope that during the. coining Lenten season a faithful attention to religious duties will be carried out by the recipients of his personal letter.

ALLIES FINDING THEIR SOUL.

The Bev. G. B. Thurston On " Character " That Counts.

In a Lenten letter to the parishioners of St. Mary's, Lymm, the Rector (the Rev. G. B. Thurston), writes : "1 feel, as I am sure you all feel, that these are no ordinary times; under the cataclysm of the Great War there is being slowly and pain- fully evolved a new 'world, in ,which, ...:under God's procidence, we shall be called upon to take our place. Great Britain, like Fraace and Russia, is finding her soul.; wonderful work in the direction of endurance, self-sacrifice, and utter devotion among the millions of men in our Navy and Army is hourly bringing this to pass. The question for us at home is—What contribution are we each individually making towards this great end? What sacri- fices? What conscious efforts? The Lenten season, as it seems to me, comes with a specially insistent voice this year, calling to every Churchman and Churchwoman to 'gird up their loins' and be active in the Lord's service for strengthening and improvement of their characters, for, after all, it is character that counts. The truest expression of what a person really is, what in him or her is the cause of their general behaviour, their most permanent aspect—this is 'character.' The development of a good character is the work God has set us in this world, a work intended to occupy us from the cradle to the grave, that which shall return to God who gave it, call it life or soul or what you will. And if I were asked what is the most essential element that we seem so much to lack in the formation of character at the present time, I should answer, unhesitatingly, inspiration or vision.

Value of Effort.

"We want -I=TO and mere .of that heavenly

===== 0002601_19160311_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
SERGEANT J. H. MOSTON.

Great Reception At Belfast For The Wounded.

Sergeant J. H. Moston, of the local "Terriers," who is at present lying in the Mater Hospital, Belfast, suffering from . nervous breakdown, writes this week to his aunt, who resides at Booth's Hill, the following letter: 'Just a few lines to let you know that I am alive and kick- ing. lam at a grand place, for everybody makes such a fuss of us. When we landed in this salnbrious spot we might have been the greatest heroes of the war. The people stopped the cars. gave us tea, biscuits and cakes, and all kinds of things. The streets were a sight, everybody was shouting 'We welcome you.' We get many outings, and someone is always wanting to take you to the Hippodrome, the pictures or a motor ride. You see it is not an Army hospital, so there are really no strict regulations to conform to. I don't know how long we shall be here. but I can tell you that after our bit in France it is divine."

===== 0002601_19160311_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE HENRY BROOKES.

Says We Shall Soon Re On Our Way

To The Rhine.

Private Henry Brookes, attached to the local "Terriers" as a stretcher-bearer; has this week written the following optimistic letter to his mother, who resides at West Hyde, Newlields " I hope you will excuse me being so long in writing .to you, but the reason is we have been continually moving about so much these last few weeks that it has been impossible. We have picked up a large draft of men as reinforce- ments during our journey, all of whom look in the picture of health and strength. A portion of them are men who have been out here with us before, but have returned fully recovered from their wounds which they received during the earlier stages of the war. It is nice to see the old boys back again. Amongst them are a few of, the Lymm fellows;‘ and all are looking particularly fit. I see niany of the married men are being called upon for. service, and prac- tically all, to my mind, will be called up by July. By that time .I hope we shall be on the way to the Rhine.- Anyhow, I think that before another three months have elapsed we shall see great changes on this front in particular. We gradually working our way back again to the war area, and shall soon be settled down to our new work. Sorry to hear, through "The Ex- aminer," of the death of S: Brazendale. He was a young fellow who was, well liked by all, although we never saw a great deal of the transport men, and I knciw, he will be missed very much by his. comradcts, both at his job and also with us in the rank'S."- Heroic Ambulance Man.

Private Brookes, it will be remembered, is a member of that patriotic family whose account we gave some few weeks ago, and is chiefly noted for , his success in winning the coveted St. George's Medal for herciic bravery in ambulance work. Although residing at Heald Green, Cheadle, he was born and brought up in Ly-mm. His stepfather, Private James Taylor, is an old Army veteran, having served in the Boer War and on gurrison duty in Gibraltar with the 2nd South Lancashires.

PADGATE.

Fire in Goods Yard.—An outbreak of fire occurred .between Sire and six o'clock on Thurs- day morning in the goods yard. at the C.L.C. Station at Padgate. The office occupied by Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., colliery pro- prietors, was in flames. The structure, which was made of wood, was an easy prey to the flames and was soon a blaze of light, which could be seen for miles around. About 6.15 the roof fell in, and shortly afterwards nothing was left but a heap of ashes.

RolfeSeouts.—A new troop of Boy Scouts has ' been organised in connection with the Padgate Wesleyan Sunday School,. with Mr. W. E. Plinston as Scout Master. On WednesdaY even- ing the Secretary of the Warrington District. (Mr. Newton), along with three Scout 11-asters and a small contingent of .Scouts from Messrs.. J. Crosfield and Sons, paid a visit to Padgate.in. order to enrol the new troop as members of the Warrington District. The swearing " in of the troop was a very interesting ceremony. Afterwards a short address was given to the boys by Mr. Newton, who complimented them on their smart appearance, and expressed the hope that in whatever sphere of life they might be placed they would not forget the promises they had rs.,dr, thnt *.i7ht hut would set an example to those with whom they came in contact. Mr. Plinston very suitably replied, at the same time expressing thanks to the visitors for the trouble they had taken to pay a visit to Paxlga,te. Wesley Guild.—The. monthly devotional meet- ing of the Wesley Guild was held on Monday evening, presided over by the Rev. A. J. Morton. A very interesting address was given by the Rev. H. T. Dixon on "Spiritual Arithmetic." He mentioned that the Bible introduces a new kind of arithmetic, and contains some of the most remarkable problems that can be found any- where. He used several illustrations from the Scriptures to prove that a careful study of the Bible reveals the fact that many ,of its pro- blems upset all the rules of arithmetic to which we have been accustomed. A Church can multi- ply by adding, and can also multiply by 'sub- tracting. As an illustration he mentioned the RomAn persecutions, at the end of which the Church emerged triumphant. It is also possible to multiply by dividing. As a result of this we have a large number of Churches all working together for one common good, and if the club organ, which we call the modern Church, is an efficient instrument, and we all do our part, we

shall ha' t 3 success. A solo was very sweetly sung by Miss A. Ball, and Miss E. A. Bennett was the accompanist.

OLD MAN'S BLOW.

£2O For Being Knocked Down During Gale.

The rough weather on New Year's Day maving flung a hoarding at an old man wheeling a bar- row in Winwick-street, an action alleging negli- gence and clailning damages was heard by Judge Scott Fox, K.C., and a jury at the County Court on Thursday, against Messrs. Dolan and Son, of Napier-street, who were at the time engaged on making extensions at Messrs. Guest Bros., tanners.

The plaintiff was Thomas Belsbaw; an out. porter, and now an inmate of the Workhouse. He was represented by Mr. W. Madden, barris- ter (instructed by Mr. C. H. Unsworth). Mr. Madden explained that Belshaw was struck by a section of a hoarding 1701 b. in weight, this having been blown from 'Messrs. Guest Bros.' works. He was knocked down and stunned, and after spending a fortnight in hos- pital, remained an out-patient for another week. Messrs. Dolan and Son, who were respon- sible for the hoarding, were extremely sorry. the accident had happened, but were trying to get out of paying for it by saying it was an' act of -God, but he contended that it had not been erected sufficiently- strongly to resist a high wind, and, therefore, there was negligence. Out-porters' Pay.

Belshaw.stated that since the accident he had tried to do light work, but he became dizzy and had to give up. On an average he used to make about 255. a week. Often he got as much as 9s. a day from commercial travellers. Mr. Thomas Curran, architect, considered that something more ought to have been done in order to resist the wind. If the hoarding had been tied with wire rope or wedged in, it would never have been blown away. -

Mr. Madden: In your opinion was it defective? Used to be Strong.

John Monks, whO' had worked with plaintiff for many years, said that Belshaw used to be a strong man, but on two occasions he had recently had to help him to a seat owing to his complaining of dizziness. For the defence, it was contended by Mr Wingate Saul (barrister) that Ow hoarding was fixed with reasonable security against bad weather, and that on New Year's Day the wind was entirely abnormal; in fact, a heavy gale was blowing. • Several witnesses were called to bear out this contention, in addition to which Dr. E. A. Fox stated that he found no evidence of any disease which was likely to cause -the plaintiff to become dizzy. The jury awarded the plaintiff £2O, and j udg. yacht was guterei.l accordingV.

- The Chief Constable's Hint To Licensees. The first prosecution in Warrington under the new licensing regulations was heard yesterday at the Police Court, before Mr. J. W. Hayward in the chair, and other magistrates. Albert Widd, licensee of the Roan Cow, beer- house, Bridge Foot, was summoned for permit- ting intoxicating liquor to be consumed on the premises after 9.30 p.m. on March Ist, Two police officers passing the defendant's house at 9.40 p.m. heard someone speaking loudly in the bar. On going inside they observed a number of men sitting round the fire with jugs in front of them containing small quantities of beer. The landlord informed the officers that he had made an effort to get the men to leave the premises, but was unsuccessful.

Mi. Browne, who defended, admitted the facts, . but said that on the night in question a big obstreperous individual known as "Jack the Giant Killer" defied the authority of the land- lord and would not ' drink up his beer. Mr. Widd went to look for a policeman to turn him out, but he could not find one. As the man was such a strapping fellow it was a physical im- • possibility for the landlord to put him. out. Some week g ago the same individual created a disturbance in the Post Office and the police wore summoned to eject him. The prosecution was taken under the new Order, and it was yet difficult for the working men to realise that they should drink up and finish at 9.30. The Chief Constable &aid he had no desire to press .the case. The landlord drew the atten- tion of the officers to the conduct of the man.

The case was dismissed on payment ot costs. the Chairman suggesting that licensees should report to the police where men defied their authority.

ItEAR-LIGHTS ESSENTIAL.

The necessity for carrying rear-lights on vehicles was emphasised at the Police Court on Monday, when several defendants were fined either 2s. 6d. or ss. each for being without these lamps on either their carts or cycles. . Generally, the defendants pleaded that they were not aware of the new regulations. The Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls) said it was exceedingly •dangerous . for cyclists and carters to be without either front' or rear-lights, especially since the new Lighting Order had been in force. The Chairman Mr. S. Wright) declared that in. future the public .must not expect to get off as lehiently as they had done that morning'.

OFFENDING MOTORISTS.

Exceed Speed Or Infringe Lighting

Regulations.

Three prosecutions for driving motor cars at a speed dangerous to the public were heard at the Police Court on Tuesday, before Mr. Henry Roberts in the chair, and other magistrates. John Fielding, 39, Forest, Oldham, was fined 20s. for an offence in Sankey-street on Wednes- day night, February 9th. The Chief Con-stable, whilst on his bicycle, noticed a heavy motor waggon pass along Sankey-street at far too great a speed. Although on his bicycle, the Chief was unable to over- take it until approaching Market Gate. He estimated the speed at twelve miles an hour, which was a source of danger now that the streets were darkened. • Defendant said it was the first time he had ever been reported for exceeding the speed limit. He passed through Warrington twice daily on his journeys between Liverpool and Oldham. For driving a motor ,cycle along Knutsford- road at a dangerous speed, George Henry Jones, Willow Bank, Cadishead, was fined Ns:

, He pleaded guilty, and Mr. T. S. Steel, who appeared on his behalf, suggested that as his client was anxious to avoid a conviction he was quite willing to give a contribution of £2 to the poor box and pay the cost of the proceedings. Barton, 15, Litchfield-street, Wigan, was ordered to pay a contribution of 13s. and 7s. costs for driving a motor cycle round Market Gate circle in a manner dangerous to the public. MOTOR FIRE ENGINES.

In recognising the pitch of excellence which motor fire engines have attained, it is a cause of satisfaction to remember that the pioneers of the modern motor fire appliance were .an English firm; namely, Messrs. Merry Weather 'arid Sons, of Greenwich, who built their first motor steam fire engine in 1899. No one will question the t the motor engine has revolutionised fire brigade Practice. Efficiency was never so ,important as in these times of hostile air raids, :and the in creased risk of fire in .factories which are run- ning at the highest pressure, on war work. Nc insurance can compensate for the delay in turn- ing out munitions which a serious fire must inevitably cause.

WHY YOU ARE

WEARING YOURSELF OUT.

All our life through, we are all wearing ourselves out—using up the substance of our bodies! Hard, laborious toil wastes us away quickly, but every expenditure of energy, every movement, every thought, causes some wastage; and this wastage should be repaired by our daily food, but that is only possible when it is well-digested. Think, then, of the im- portance of keeping your digestive organs in good working order, especially in these, days when we are called upon to give the most that is in.us. If for any reason your organs of digestion, the stomach, liver and bowels, fail to perform their functions perfectly, there is no remedy- that will so soon restore them to health and vigour as Mother Seigel's Syrup. As a stomachic remedy and liver corrective, it would be difficult to find its equal.
