I. OW LS.

Last Year's Generosity To Be Repeated.

GAMES FOR CHARITY.

Like a cluster of excited spectators around a "close end" pri a green, delegates to the annual meeting of the War- rington Works' Amateur Bowling Associa- tion crowded round the huge fire in the spacious club room at the Horse and Jockey, Winwick-road, on Monday evening. It was a cold, wintry night, and the delegates discussed enthusiastically the summer game of bowls. Mr. John Willett presided, and Mr. W. Patrick (hon. secretary) reviewed the work of the year, first eulogising the work of Lieut.- Colonel Crosfield at the Front, and going* on to say that last year they had another successful season, despite the upset which the war caused to all bowling associations. It,.t.ud given the epzumittee and, officials inereasea work and anxiety, but they could congratulate themselves upon the success , attained. In the Finlayson Cup Competition there was a falling off in entries, owing to the great number of members who had t enlisted. Forty' teams entered, as against, seventy-two in 19j4 and PeariOn. and Knowles were the winners.' In regard to the Merit Competition, that was hardly the success they had hoped for, and they trusted that in future the players for whom the competition was prepared would back up the committee. All played off scratch, and the shield given by the President was worthy of a place in any bowler's home. • Mr. A. Gleave, jun., of Crosfield's, proved the winner, by defeat- ing Mr. H. Bennion„ of Whitecross, unmerei- I fully in the final game. Playing For Charity.

Continuing, Mr. Patrick .aid their Pre.vident had requested that iho association should lie as low as possible, unless their bowling were for charity. They had lived up to this by

organising war fund handicaps. Every ple‘nni taken in entrance fees had been devoted to local institutions for the benefit of soldiers. No one knew where the prizes were coming from. ' In peace times the first thing a bowler wanted to know was: "What are we playing for?" but not so with these handicaps. Players came rolling up, so that out of the first tournament they.were able to hand over the handsome sum of £3O, as well as purehase footballs te."-the.value of £3 for the lads at the Front. "Not so bad for a start," commented Mr. Patrick.

The second competition was hardy so sucess- ful, but they were able to hand over Jo the Mayor £2l for the Prisoners Of War Fund.

• The association, along with the Caledonian Association, assisted by various local insti tn. bons, ariganged a bowling carnival on the War- ririgton Cricket Ground, as a result of which (.150 was handed over to local institutions. How To Be Suecessful This Year.

R ..gareing the coming season, he said it wou*.ti be more trying than that of 1915, owing to many usmi:hcrs be•ng called up for service left t.tai ri4ht lie was often asked where they were t ) the players from to run the t. petitions. His answer was: do as they did in 1915—play for the boys away; give all support to provide comforts for them, give small prizes and large donations, and they were assured of success. (Applause.) Mr. 'Burdett submitted the accounts, which showed that the income for the year was £157 10s., and they had a balance of4lB in the bank. There had been a falling off in subscriptions, he said. and he was rather afraid that with in- r.eased 'iticorne tax .and the higher ,price of, :egmrnoilities•they :would have to face a further ieduction this year.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
OFFICIALS RE-ELECTED.

Lieutenant-Colonel Crosfield, who has been president of the association for many years, was re-elected, as was also Mr. John Willett to the chairmanship. The latter remarked that many of their young men might have to leave them. but whenever they were playing the fine old game the thoughts of those at home would con- tinually go out towards them. (Applause.) Mr. Patrick was re-elected secretary, Mr. Burdett treasurer, and Messrs. B. Straw and T. Horrabin auditors. Eulogies of each were pro- nounced.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
REDUCTION IN SIZE OF TEAMS.

On the suggestion of the Firth Mattress Co., Ltd., and the Wire Ropery, it was decided to confine the number of players in the Finlayson Cup Competition to eight, instead of twelve, and have a reserve of four. It was explained that some works would have a difficulty in raising a larger team than eight, swing to enlistments. It was also decided to make the handicap limit six instead of seven, the casting vote of the Chairman effecting this alteration. One or two novices objected to the reduction,' remarking that the committee could decide whether a man ought to have as high a handicap as seven.

"Unfortunately, the committee cannot keep its eyes on all the 1,600 or 1,700 members we have got," replied Mr. Patrick. "Not in- frequently a man gets a better start than he should do."

Mr. Willett :•" With a start of seven, it means that if your opponent makes a mistake you can be home almost immediately in a game of fifteen up." _ •

The committee elected were: H. Raiford, Wire Itopery; F. Dean, Whitecross ; R. Smith, County Asylum; J. Howarth, Naylor's; T. Humphries. Rylands'; J. Ashton, Firth Mat- tress: J. Spencer. P. and K.; W. Bridge, Cheshire Lines; J. Smith, Richmond's; P. Lunt, Howley Tannery': and Jas. Davies, Crosfield's.

It was this abnormal bouncing of the ball which once deceived Aspinall in anticipating a pass from Broster when far afield. Had he jumped up to it he might have prevented Silcock, a smart left full-back, relieving and beating back the attack.

Although Warrington pressed forward again and again, they failed to score in the first half, several promising efforts fizzling out. Early on Aspinall and Foster burst through brilliantly, and the latter tested Ellison, the custodian, with a point-blank shot, which he stopped well.. Three times our forwards passed the backs and had an open goal before them, but nothing nunerical eneuecl. Exciting moments merely became disappointing memories. Three tithes were hundred to one chances missed. Why? The Town spectators could not comprehend, but I afterwards leftrned that just7outside the goal there is a slimy patclk which caused them to lose control over their' feet and pull them up, or, if they attempted to shoot, they could not help „1:)ut go astray:' Thus:: mysterious and disap pointing developments had a simple explana- tion. But the referee allowed • it. This wig twenty-five minutes after the re-start.

Warrington refused to bow the knee, and persisted, with the result that, after smart work: on the left and a swing over to the right, Bros- ter sent in a swift shot from a moderate range, and a goal accrued. There were only five min- utes to go, and the Town put in their best, but they failed to get the .equaliser, though when the whistle blew they were still attacking.

Accidents—resulting in the weakening and the disorganisation of the forward line—and some slippery earth just in front of the home goal in the first half, accounted for the defeat. Little blame could be attached to the individual players.

INTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

Only One Match.

Th--;--o intermediate Rugby League matches should have taken place on Saturday, but St. Mary's failed to fulfil their engagement with Parochial. The other match, however, that of St. Thomas' v. Bolton. was a fine one, and after 'leading almost throughout the game, victory' was wrested from the grasp of St. Thomas' in the last few minutes.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
A THRILLING FINISH.

. A good number of spectators, including some. wounded soldiers,-. witnessed a' keen 'and.-excit- ing, game, with' a thrilling finish, at Wilder - pool on Saturday. The opposing teams were St. Thomas' Bolton, and until two minutes from time the former team were leading by three points to two. Bolton had not given up :trying, however, ,and their.,persisteney was. at rewarded, for Jones,, receiving the ball' at the half-way line, made a fine run along the right wing. Arrived at about a dozen yards from the line, and probably feeling he had not enough strength for the final effort, he gave to Worthington, who, with a fine dash, cut through the defence and scored far out, amidst great enthusiasm on the part of the Bolton followers. Wilcoeks failed at goal, but that mattered not, for the. whistle sounded for time immediately afterwards, leaving Bolton the victors by five points to three. Throughout the game the play had been equally divided amongst the respective teams. St. Thomas' won the toss,. and, if anything, proved themselves rather the better team in the first half. Their centre-threequarters were smart, but Jones paid them every attention and executed, some fine . tackling, besides initiating many a promising passing movement. Half-way through the first half a good 'passing movement by the St. Thomas' backs nearly ended in. disaster for the ,Boltonitesbut the player was pushed into touch just in time. The bakl. was 'worked away, however, and, when in the vicinity of St. Thomas' line,' the Bolton lads were awarded a free-kick for obstruction, but Jones failed at goal. A few minutes later they were bac,,k again and were awarded, an- 'other free-kick far out. The kick this time was given to Wileocks, who, with a fine hefty effort, scored a good goal. Brown Pinches a Try.

Nettled by this reverse, St. Thomas' bore down on their opponents' line, and here the ball became loose. With lightning-like rapidity Brown snapped it up, and before' the defenders realised their position, he was over the line. The same player essayed the kick at goal, but the ball struck the crossbar and rebounded into play. This was the position of affairs at half- time. . _

On resuming, it .vas qUite evident that Bolton meant business, and on several occasions were almost over. St. Thomas', however, defended gamely, and, as time drew near, the popular opinion was that they would retire victorious. Indeed, many of the spectators had left the field, expecting there would be no further scoring. When least expected, however, there came the movement which won the victory, and made amends for Bolton's defeat of a try to nil which they sustained a fortnight ago at the hands of the lads from Stockton Heath.

Friendly matches have been arranged with Widnes Intermediate League for Good Friday at Warrington, and on Easter Monday at Widnes.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT JUNIOR LEAGUE.

P. W. L. D. F. A. P. St. Elphin's Orford Juniors Howley "United Penketh Juniors Fairfield United Res. 10 6 3 1 27 16 11 Stockton Heath Scouts 11 . 4 6 1 17 30 9 Bewsey A.F.C. Mersey Juniors Penketh Juniors, Howley United and Mersey Juniors have each had two points deducted for 'playing ineligible players.

As was generally anticipated, the meeting of the Fairfield United Reserves and St. Elphin's was a keenly contested game. On many occasions the. players allowed the excitement to get the upper hand, and their exhibition of the passing game was not of the standard usually given by these teams. With the exception of Holden, the Reserves were at full strength, and they started with confidence and played very strongly. For twenty minutes the home team had by far the best of the game. The visitors then improved in their play, but never looked like scoring in the first half. Lygo was success- fu! for the home team a few minutes before the interval.

In the second portion the " Saints " tried to infuse more energy into their game, but Rigby was their only dangerous forward, and he re- ceived very little assistance from his colleagues, Woodhouse at centre-half for the United, break- ing up many attempts by the "Saints." Lygo was a source of trouble to the visitors' defence, and he succeeded in adding the second goal. It was after the second reverse that the •" Saints" showed their best efforts and Kidd, in the United goal, had several good shots to deal with. A splendid shot by Shelmerdine found the net. Woodhouse, in attempting to clear, diverted the course of the ball out of the reach of the goalkeeper.

A burst away to the "Saints"' end 'saw Lock.. hart make ,a grand save from Lygo; and then the" Saints" made a final effort-to get,ort equal terms, but they had left it till it was too late, the final whistle sounding with the result in favour of the United by two goals against one.

The United deserved their success on the day's play. „Their forwards were more consistent in their methods, and their defence played a very strong game. The half-back line proved too good for the opposing forwards.

The " Saints " did not show championship form at all. Lockhart could not be blamed for the shots that beat him. The backs only showed moderate form. Miller, at centre-half, was the most prominent player of the " Saints' " team, and Rigby was their most dangerous for- ward.

On the Penketh Recreation. i Ground, the Mersey Juniors, who were short of three a their usual players, played a "very good game against the home team.. No goals were scored in the first portion, but in the second half the home team were playing their favourite way, down the slope, ana visiting teams to the Rec. gener- ally find it out. The home team scored on three occasions, and the visitors failed to respond. White, Sankey and Thomasson all showed good form for the hemei team. ,

The return fixture ba-ween,Howley United and Orford Juniors was' played on the Arpley ground under conditions which proved disastrous to the United. Owing to a protest they were not able to include Hough, Miller and Bradburn in their eleven, and it was with difficulty that substi- tutes could be found. Ogden, the secretary. made up the team. The Juniors had their best team out, and had the best of the exchanges throughout the game, leading at the interval by two goal to one. In the second half they found the net on' three more occasions, while the home team replied twice, the result being in favour of Orford by five goalsi-against three

This. victory to the Juniors lifts them to second ,place in the 'League chart.

Davis scored twice from penalty kicks.

WARRINGTON TOWN F:C.

The Daintith Incident.

To the Editor of "The Exam4ler."

Sir,--A perusal ;of the letter from Mr. Peter Peacock, jun., in your-issue of Saturday, last, must convince your readers of the hopeless position he finds himself in, and to draw them from the facts he now resorts to a window- dressing process. Childish though the letter is—the references to myself being amusing—l think it as well to again expose his inaccuracies. In my previous letter I endeavoured to make it quite clear that the rules of the Football Association would not allow Daintith to play for the Town Club, hut Mr. Peacock, jun., now asserts that "there are no rules binding any player, amateur or professional, during the present crisis." If his statement is correct, why was it necessary to trouble about a "permit"? His contention is absurd.

I Mr. F. J. Wall, the secretary of the Football lAssociation', in a reply to a letter from me on the subject, definitely states that the memoran- dum quoted in my letter of the- 26th ult., has not been varied for the present season, and is still operative. Surely, as clever as Mr. Pea- cock, jun., would have us believe he is, he -would not have the fake to suggest that the secretary of the Football Association is wrong. I can only repeat that Dainlith did not play for the Town Club at his own request. The player personally informed me that he was approached by Mr. Peacock, jun., and asked to play for them, and only consented when he was informed that he (Mr. Peacock, jun.) had seen the secretary of the Warrington Club and that it was all' right. The letter from Daintith re- gretting the misunderstanding was also written at the same person's request. If the Town Club's official will conduct the club's business on constitutional lines there will be no necessity to remind him of the Association's rules and regulations, and it will probably be the means of helping him to play the game instead of resorting to gallery tactics. —Yours, &c.,

T. P. BRADSHAW, Hon. Secretary Warrington F.C.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKING MEN'S ASSOCIATION.

N.A. CENTRE.

There was more than the usual muster at the Gowy last week-end, owing to the weather somewhat taking up and the nearness of the closing season for coarse' WI. Chester, Chirk, Waverton, and Afickle Trafford were all visited, with poor results, except for the members who visited Waverton (not the canal at Waverton, the upper reaches of .the Gowy). Here there were fair catches. but not of the quality that are taken lower down.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOWY.

The fishing of this favourite stream this winter has proved very 'disappointing, the heaviest roach falling to J. Green; 'weight, 11b. ' loz. Taking other years up to about this date there is always up to half a dozen roach creeled up to 11b. Anglers who have followed the water up this year have never caught it in con- dition. Take, for instance, last week-end. The weather in Warrington appeared all that was desired for the Gowy angler, but upon arriving on the scene on Sunday there was a downfall of snow during the early part of the morning, and it was not long before the water from it began to run in the stream, causing the eddies and slacks to boil, and by 3 p.m. almost all anglers had given up their chance for one more week. There is only one more week-end left for those who generally hold up their .end at the Gowy. They have something on to beat last year's roach of 11b. 7oz.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER PLACES.

The best of the other waters, if there has been any best, has been the Shropshire Union Canal, where roach have reached about nos. Norton has, been fished very little, and this is the place for the novice to get amongst the specials. The river at Chester has yielded some fair' bream, but none came Warrington way. The canal at Waverton has fared no better than the same water at Chester. The Gowy at Waverton end has yielded "fish to anglers, but they are lack- ing in size, and one asks, Is it worth the journey for such?

===== 0002601_19160311_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 11, 1916.

OFFENDING TRADESMEN.

Ten Fined For Having TOO Much. Light.

Four more shopkeepers and six market tenants have paid the penalty for infringing the lighting regulatipns. To business men who invoke the aid of the police officers to instruct them: as to their duties in carrying_ out the regulations, the Chairman (Mr. Hy. Roberts), at the. Police Court on _Tues- day, laid it down that it was no part of the duty .of the .police to give such initructions. It was the duty of everyone to obey the Lighting Order, and they had 4ght to put their own respon- sibility On the shoulders of an official.. The lighting Of the stalls in the Market also came in fOr criticism, and the Opinion was expressed by the Chairman that there was no real reason why the Market at this particular time should be lighted. As many people received their.. wages on Friday and all on Saturday, surely it was possible for people .to do their shopping long before lights were necessary. •

===== 0002601_19160311_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
EIGHT RESPONDENTS.

Mr. Hy. Thornton's Application At County Court Fails.

An unusual application under the War Emer- gency Powers Act, .which failed, • was heard by Judge. Scott Fox, K.C., at the County Court on Thursday, when Mr. E. A. White, appearing on behalf of Mr. Henry Thornton, of Fearn- head Hall, Warrington, asked for leave to distrain for rent of 1:35 upon the goods of the owners and occupiers of four houses in May- field-road, Grappenhall, he being the landowner. The respondvlts were,: Samuel Rowland, of The Anchorage, Mayfield-road, Grappenhall; Frank Leach, 75, Walton-road, Stockton Heath; Joseph Barrett Wood and .Vanda Wood, of Northdene, Thelwall-road, Grappenhall; David Maile, of Halton Cottage, ,Mayfield-road, Grap- penhall.; Edna Florence, Rowland, of The Anchorage, Mayfield-road, Grappenhall; Mrs. Wright, of Newlands, Mayfield-road; and Wil- liam Buckby, of Greeba, Mayfield-road. Mr. A. Browne appeared for all respondents, excepting Leach and Millie, for the first' of whom Mr. T. S. Steel was present. Maile was unrepresented. • Mr. White explained that this was an appli- cation to establish Mr. Thorntmes title to the land. rather .than for leave to, issue distress, thinking that this was the best. nif.tams to he taken.

Mr. Browne intervened with the remark that really it was an action for the Chancery Court. Working Men v. Millionaire.

Mr. White proceeded to say that Lord Barry. more leased a large plot of land to a Mr. Roberts in Grappenhall; who sub-leased a por- tion to a Mr. Griffiths, and. Mr. Thornton sub- sequently became the owner of such land by different assignments. Mr. Browne told his Honour that his clients disputed that they owed the money and re- minded him that he had no right to hold whether Mr. -Thornton was entitled to distrain.

His Honour : I am going to leave that abso- lutely unprejudiced. Mr. Browne: You leave us in this lamentable position: we are practically working men and have to fight a millionaire His honour: I have not made a working man or millionaire. _ '

His Honour agreed that he had no right to go into the deeds of title and would only call upon the respondents and ask whether they had been affected by the war, leaving the risk to the applicant. The first respondent' called was Samuel Row- land, and Mr. Browne said he had no interest whatever in the property and was a builder and joiner seriously affected by the war. Mr. White: If Mr. Rowland has no interest in it then I will withdraw his name.

_ His Honour: Then you ilbandon the whole. Lengthy legal arguments ensued,. with the result that his Honour dismisseethe summons, without costs. •. *".-` '

CRICKET CLUB.

Mr. T. Locker And Difference Between Germans And British.

At the annual meeting ;of the Warrington Cricket, Bowling and Lawn Tennis Club, new on Thursday night at the,Lion Hotel, it was 'decided to acknowledge the • patriotiSm of the thirty .members of the club who have joined the Forces, by the provision of. a roll of honour in the club house.

Alderman Tinnion, who brought forward the suggestion, said the war would be over 801110 day, and it would be a source of great pride to them to know that friends of the club had answered the country's call; and had done their share in fighting Our battles in the most terrible ,struggle the world had ever seen. It was the least they could do to provide, a roll of honour. The proposal was accepted, the arrangements to be left to the committee. „

_ An exceedingly satisfactory balance sheet was sulamitted by Mr. John Rigby, the treasurer. The season began with a debit balance of £2B 12s. lid., and at the end of the year they had a balance in hand of £97 14s: Bd. The expendi- ture was less than the income by nearly £l3O. This was owing almost entirely to the absence of cricket during the war. They had had no professional cricketer to phy and no cricket match expenses. The committee had decided to take the management of the ground entirely in their own hands, and a full time groundsman had been appointed. This would involve an in- creased expenditure, but it was .hoped that the improvement in the ground. would -be com- mensurate with the increased cost,-

A vote of thanks to the President (Mr. E. Wallington) for his services during the year was proposed by Mr. G. Brown, and seconded by Mr. T. Cooper. On the proposition of Mr. E. Wallington, seconded by Alderman Tinnion, Mr. T. Locker wa elected president for the coming season. THE NEW PRESIDENT.

Mr. Thos. Locker, in responding, said the only condition for admission to the club was that the applicant should be a "sport." There lay th., fundamental difference between themselves as a people and their national foes. The Germans did not understand what the mental and moral spirit was that lay behind the scorn and hatred they felt for treachery, Cruelty and striving to win by every foul means in preference to fair. The following officers were elected :—Secretary, Mr. C. IL Unsworth; treasurer. Mr. John Rigby; cricket secretary, Mr. B. Silcock ; tennis secretary. Mr. Bayly Isaac; bowling secretary, Mr. T. Cooper; bowling captain, Mr. R. T. Fairclough ; cricket captain, Mr. W. Richard- son; sub-captain, Mr. S. L. Partridge; Second Team captain, Mr. W. V. Turton. The general committeee, in. addition to ex-officio members:— Bowling, Messrs. W. S. Haddock, G. Brown, J. F. Forster, K. Bradford; cricket, Messrs. W. Richardson, S. L. Partridge, B. Silcock and W. V. Tnrton; tennis section, E. J. Byerley and C. Price.

The committce are considering a proposal to allow the ie of the bowline' green to tha:i wounded soldiers stationed in Warrington dur- ing the summer months.

SOCIALIST lIALL.

At the adjourned licensing sessions yesterday, On the application of Mr. T. S. Steel, the dancing and music licence of the Socialist Hall, Bridge Foot, which was refused at the last sessions, was renewed on the stipulation that the premises closed at 10.30 p.m It was pointed out by Mr. Steel that thirty-one members of the Warrington. branch of the .British Socialist Society had voluntarily offered their services to the Military authorities for the Army. The society had always been willing to allow the room to. be used for charitable purposes, and a sum of over 00 had been raised for various war charities. He (Mr. Steel) believed there was an impression abroad that a certain person who had made himself very obnoxious was associated with the society. , Such was not correct, as the person had no connection with the society, as he was cleared-out some fifteen months ago. The members of the society were Blatchfordian in their views with regard to the war. There was a section who followed the ideas' of Mr. Ramsay Macdonald, but with those views his clients were net associated. • The, society had suspended 'their Socialistic propa- ganda work. • The licence was renewed.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
AN EFFICIENT FLEET.

Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., Says Navy Is Still "Our All-in-All."

CONSERVATIVE ASSOCIATION

Mr. Harold Smith, • M.P., who., •as is - well- known, is serving in the R.N.R., has written a reassuring letter regarding the efficiency of our Fleet. It was read at the an'. :al meeting of the Conservative and Unionist 7Association, held at the Club, in Sankey--street, on Thursday even- ing, and stated:— • • - " You know that I have some opportunity of 'acquainting myself with matters affecting our Fleet. Let no sinister rumour be listened to in Warrington. There are, of course, no two opinions about the gallant men who 'handle and man our ships. There are, however, .mysterious suggestions occasionally put forward to the effect that we .are not taking adequate steps- to increase our materiel and generally to ensure our ability to continue the ' elean'Sweep ' policy. . " Let no 'such thoughts - disturb you. . I am not at liberty to say more than to 'reassure you' that' there is no sort of foundation for these doubts, and that we may rest quiet with the full knowledge that our Fleet is still our all in all,' and will so remain." The optimistic reassurance of the letter was most welcome, and members applauded heartily.

GERMANS HAVE GONE NAP ON WRONG CARDS.

Mr. W. P. Rylands also had a few words to say on the subject. He essayed that we started the 'war with one great advantage. We were what one might call„" Professionals" in regard to the Navy, although " amateurs " as far as having a large Army was concerned. Natur- ally, in the Army there had been a good Many mistakes—just the sort amateurs usually made. But with regard to the Navy we had pro- fessionals, and their 'conduct must, be judged by results. The Fleet was absolutely as perfect as a machine could be, and was in. the hands of the best men in the whole world. (Applause.) " It is perfectly true that the Germans may be doing something," he added. "We do not know. But let us not wring our hands about it until we know what it is, and let us be assured that the Germans, like everybody else, cannot hold a quart in a pint pot." (Laughter, and hear, hear.) Despite the fact of the enemy's power of organisation, Mr. Rylands contended that they must be labouring under great difficulties in their workshops owing to having sent so many men to the Front. They had also concentrated on making submarines—in fact, they had "gone nap" on them—and it must have been to the neglect of other sections of the Navy.

SIR GILBERT AND THE NEXT ELECTION. In the report of the Hon. Secretary (Mr George Smith) it was stated that they must 'not forget the members of the association who had sacrificed their lives in the country's cause, and it was hoped fivit in time a fitting memorial would bE provided.

Naturally, there had been a cessation of party politic:. but expenses have been • incurred in carrying on th. Tyropaganda, and in pr.& vitling comforts for soldier 3. . Mr E•iwin iiiadshaw oon. treasurer), said that hitherto it had been his pleasure to say that the finances of the association were in a good condition, but lie could not say the same this year. Last year he made an appeal to members to subscribe as, liberally as possible, and he was sorry it had not met with a better response. He hoped that the business carried on by the association in place of political pro- paganda would cause members to spare some- thing, so that it could be continued.

In- supporting this appeal, • Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart.. who presided, remarked that although no political work was being done, they would have to face an election ,sooner or later, and they wanted to be in fighting form. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160311_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMANLY MODESTY.

Archdeacon's Concern For Girls Who Work.

Dealing, with various iorms of sensual sin, the Archdeacon of Chester (the Yen. W. L. Paige Cox)•in an address to women in the War- rington Parish Church, yesterday afternoon, asked whether it was not necessary that we should keep -up the standard of -womanly modesty amongst our growing girls? Girls, nowadays, he said, lived • lives •so differently to years ago. There had been a change. They went out to work and so it would be when the war was over, for it was in their interest that they should have independencies. Therefore, it was for all who had an influence over young girls to teach them to have self- respect, modesty and self-restraint in all their habits, especially in their relations with young men.

The reverend gentleman also warned them about attending, theatits and picture houses regularly, for occasionally there, came before their 'eyes something which was objectionable. In that case he thought Christian women should walk out as a protest. Speaking of alcoholic drinks, the preacher de- clared that while our religiohn did not teach us that it was wrong to take Intoxicants, it had been pointed out at the beginning of the war, especially in the interests of soldiers, that strict temperance made for phvical efficimey; and he thought all people should be total abstainers. He also appealed to those present to make their homes attractive and so reduce the temptation their men folk had of going into public-houses for enjoyment and peace. BISHOP OF LICHFIELD'S ADDRESS TO

===== 0002601_19160311_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
MEN.

Last night the Bishop of Lichfield addressed a vast congregation of men in. the Parish Church, and • spoke of the double war which was now going on—the war between the Allies and Ger- many. and the _war against evil, selfishness, and self-will of all kinds. The great bone for the future, he said, was that the spirit of self- sacrifice, service and adventure being so splen- didly shown in Flanders and elsewhere. should be enlisted in the cause of the Kingdom of God.

KNOCKED DOWN BY TAXI.

Coroner's Sympathy For Drivers In

The Dark.

A few hours after attending the funeral of a boy who had been drowned, Harry Buckley (46), Canal Side Cottages, Grappenhall, was knocked down by a motor ear and received in- juries from which he died • in the Warrington Infirmary on Thursday. •

The inquest was field at the Warrington Police Station on Friday afternoon. It-was stated by John Wilfred Paterson, 741, Knutsford-road, that as he was driving his taxi along Knutsford-road, Grappenhall, near the Dog and Dart Inn, he noticed the deceased walking in the roadway a foot in front of him. It was too dark to see him before. He was wily proceeding at six or seven miles an hour: He sounded his horn and tried to pull up, but the near wing struck Buckley. In answer: to the Coroner, the .witness said his licence was absolutely clean, although he had been driving for thirteen years. He had certainly had an accident, but he himself was awarded .Cl9O damages. The Coroner: That is a very proud record. Ilt) added that it was very unfair to drivers of motor cars and horses and traps, for pedestrians to walk in the road when it was dark. if there was a footpath. Molesters In The Dark.

_ One of the witnesses, Miss Marguerite Ellis, a parlourmaid, who happened to be in the witness-box when the Coroner made that com- ment, remarked that she was in the roadway, but she was walking by herself. The Coroner: 'Why walk in the road? Witness: In view of the things that happen on that road. The Coroner: What is that? • / Witness: We are afraid of being molested by men in the dark. Verdict: *.' Accidental death; "eriver .exoner- ated from blame."' Mr. T. S. Steel appeared • for the widow and Mr. Percy Jordan for Paterson,

===== 0002601_19160311_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Some 'War Music At Next Week's Concert For IVar Funds.

The programme of music to be performed at the Warrington Musical Society's concert on Wednesday next will be miscellaneous in character and will include some items not inappropriate to present .times and circum- stances. A special feature will be the famous "Carillon." The poem deals with the woes of grief-stricken Belgium, and was originally written :in French by the Belgian poet, M. Emile Cammaerts. On this oecasion it will be recited in English by Miss Lucy natal,. the vocal soloist of the evening. Sir Edward Elgar has written the music to accompany the reci- tation of this soul-stirring poem, and the music is already recognised and accepted as some- thing more than an ordinary example of the composer's genius. The bells—of which there are four—are used with wonderful effect -throughout the work. The ' Carillon" was first performed in December, 1914, under the conductorship of, Sir. Edward Elgar, at a con- cert of the London Symphony Orchestra at the Queen's Hall, London, when it was received with so, much enthusiasm .by the crowded audience that a second performance was imme- diately arranged .and given in the same hall. Two very interesting and appropriate short works will be given .by the society's chorus and orchestra. One of them—Sir Charles Villiers 'Stanford's "Last Post "—contains some very beautiful and expressive music. At the con- elusion a touching effect is obtained when thel "Last Post" is sounded on a trumpet in the far, far distance. The other—" The Eve of Waterloo" (words by Byron, music by H. J. Taylor)—is a very bright, inspiriting and descriptive_ work. • _

In addition to the above-mentioned attrac- tions, Miss Lucy Nuttall, the celebrated con- tralto singer, will sing three songs, one of which is the popular "When You Come Home," by Squire. There will also be some piano solos (one With :.orchestra) by Miss Marguerite Stilwell, some -violin solos (again, one with orchestra) by Mr. Alfred • Ross, an overture (" Raymond"), and a selection (" Our Empire ") by the orchestra, and a number of part-songs by members of the chorus.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0246.txt | included=true | included =====
Money.

CURRENT TOPICS

FROM

STAGELAND.

By " FOYER."

Royal Court; Good gracious! Here we are in March and one finds pantomime still going strong. After a protracted season one can become bored of too much pantomime, but remembering the good fare- provided by Harry Russell's "13°-peep" two seasons ago at the Royal Court, I looked in at his show— Cinderella" —on Thursday evening .to see. how ,things wero progressing.

found the ',present production equally as attractive and one which,. is delighting the patrons of Sir Joseph Beecham's house. Russell's productions have always stood for the itelllo of cleanliness, which, to quote the comedian's own words, `f Always pays in the long run." •- .• • ,

" Pickles " is a personal friend of mine and it was -appropriate my calling on him on the anniversary of his forty-first birthday. I was fortunate enough to participate in the cele- bration of his natal day, for, amongst other good feasts, he had many racy reminiscences to relate in his usual cheery and breezy manner. Here is one:

‘`. I was travelling by chance aShort time ago in a railway compartment with two ladies, who evidently had seen. my show at one of the theatres. One of the women said: 'Oh, Sarah, have you seen "Pickles" this week at the -.---!' 'Aye!' replied, the other; he's very funny, but them there, comedians aint much class off the stage.' "Oh! I &nine,' said the first speaker; • " Pickles" called for a drink in a pub. where I was the other evening and he spoke like, a real gent.' When Larrived at my destination, before leaving the compartment I said to one of the ladies: Excuse me, madam, if you would like to see "Pickles" again he is ap- pearing at the handed her my private card. 'Nuff said!"

The title of next week's entertainment at least sounds all right, doesn't it? "Pretty Darlings" really ought to be good enough for even the most fastidious. Evidently it refers to the bevy of beautiful ladies in this revue. I hear the show is particularly bright and refreshing.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
Hippodrome.

_ _ Monday evening was a special night at the Hippadrome, for it was the occasion of the opening of the new stalls, an innovation which reflects great credit on Mr. W. A. Jackson, the popular manager, who has always considered the coinfort of his many patrons. I saw Several of the Macnaghten directors there, who ex- pressed the opinion that the new entrance was a decided boon.

'The premier place this week must be awarded to the turn of the Sutcliffe Family, acrobat: and bag-pipers. Mr. Alfred Sutcliffe, one of the troupe, was especially interesting, for I learned that during their career they had journeyed upwards Of one Million miles and had played iu practically every country under the sun.

On six occasions have they toured Austria and Germany, having experienced many exciting moments. Their act was once attached to Barnum and Bailey's Circus, and whilst with them in Central Germany they met with all alarming railway disaster. The damage to horses and other animals was appalling and several lives were lost.

Mr. Sutcliffe showed me a photograph taken by himself of the fortifications near Kiel, from which he was chased for three miles. He evaded capture by disguising himself as a ballet girl when the police investigated.

This Scottish troupe have played before the Emperor Josef of Austria, as well as the late Archduke, the Kaiser, and the Crown Prince of Germany. "Wilhelm did not appreciate the bagpipes," laughingly remarked Mr. Sutcliffe. "The Emperor of Austria, by the way, was extremely kind and courteous; so was Little Willie."

I like the bill next week, for included in the programme are several renowned artistes. Hill, Cherry and Hill, in "Grotesque Oddities," borne with a big reputation, as also does Will Collinson, the Cockney comedian.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0248.txt | included=true | included =====
Star Kinema.

„* * * * * * * With the aid of my typesetting friend I am able admirably to convey the meaning of the films at present being screened at this popular cinema house, for those who like this particu- lar class of entertainment just now are being shown all the "star” films, which embraces the higher art of cinematography.

On Tuesday evening I particularly enjoyed a fine film in "Seven Sisters," featuring Miss Marguerite Clark as the mischievous " It is an admirable comedy, based on a Polish marriage custom, and a film so pleasing and refined that I look with pleasurable anticipa- tion to a return Visit of this: highly diverting comedy.

Have you seen " Zaza," with Miss Pauline Frederick in the title-role, appearing as these notes are being written? Miss Frederick needs no introduction to cinema-goers, for we can never forget her splendid acting and portrayal in "The Etthmal City," which appeared at the "Star" a little while ago. She is equally fine in the present production, so—" To-night's the Night" to see "The Girl on the Film."

Three notable triumphs of film production are booked for next week in "I'm Glad My Boy Grew Up To Be (A. Soldier," "Her Uncle" (by W. W. Jacobs, the famous novelist), and "Carmen." I can vouch for them being parti- cularly fine.

I cannot close without remarking upon that most sensational of films, "Carmen," which appears during the latter part of next week. This triumph of cinematography is produced by the Lasky Co., who, according to public and Press opinion, have never before produced such a gorgeous spectacle as that of "Carmen."

Adapted from the play of the same name, there is in the bull-fight scene actually an audience of 20,000, and sanction had to be obtained by ,the producers from the Brazilian Government for the affair ,to take place.

The costumes worn are identical to those worn at the present moment in "Sunny Spain," and the scenery and stage setting so elaborate that it took an army of workers months to perform their allotted tasks.

THE PRUDENTIAL.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
Stupendous Figures Of The Great Assurance Company.

The annual report of the Prudential Assur- ance Co., which appears in to-day's "Examiner," reveals some extraordinary totals with continued prosperity. Each week Since the war started, says the " Insurance Mail," the company has paid large sums in settlement of claims under policies on those who havolost their lives as a result of the hostilities. At a. recent date, the .sums so paid readied the enormous total of nearly .f.',800,000. For the vast majority of those- hundreds of thousands who held its policies prior.to the out- break of war, it has covered the heavy risks of the firing line without charging an extra penny in premiums: It has made up the salaries month by month of those thousands of its em- ployes who have joined His Majesty's Forces, Never in the, Prudential's remarkable history has it made so large an increase in its premium income as it has made in the year just closed. The increase is marvellous, and inasmuch as the Prudential is looked upon by even rivals as the standard by which the public's confidence in industrial-ordinary assurance may be judged, it must bring a glow of pride to every person connected .with the business. Clearly, insurance stands higher to-day in public esteem than ever it stood before. Popular loans have been issued by the Government at high rates of interest. Far, from the savings of the people being diverted from the Prudential thereby, more money has flowed into its coffers. The Govern- ment, also, has confidence in the Prudential. At the present time the chairman of the company is serving on a Government Committee to con- sider possibilities of economy in Ann.; expendi- ture; its generlal manager is serving on a GO,- ernment Committee connected with economy in National 'lnsurance; its actuary has served on one Government Committee dealing with popu- larising War Loans amongst the working classes, and is now serving on another; and its secretary is the manager of the Government's scheme for mobilising millions of pounds' worth of Ameri- can securities, so that the rate of the American Exchange shall be maintained. . A special point is made o• the fact that Hip bonus is not missed this year, but is postponed for later division. What the Prudential report discloses, says the "Insurance Record," is that it could undoubtedly have distributed a very large bonus to its .ordinary policyholders, but it has decided, more wisely, to hold over all such distribution. By so acting it offers not a less, but a mach greater attraction to intending policyholders, who can confidently look forward to much greater bonuses in the future than they otherwise could have 'done."

The Warrington branch of the. company, of which Mr. S. Brocklehurst ig the superintendent. has had its full share in the successful work of the past year. During 1915 no fewer. than 1.008 claims were paid over the Warrington office counter, eovering a sum of £17,178. _

- Under the Approved Society 6,151 weekly bene- fit of sickness claims were paid for £2,856, which included 304 maternity cases. These figures show the strength of the representation of the company. .

We:believe all the Warrington staff of mili- tary 'age have ,attested under Lord Derby's scheme. several already being On aetive cervice. One. ,Mr. J. Johnson,--was recently killed in action.

===== 0002601_19160311_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
"PRESSED INTO SERVICE."

A Correction And Explanation.

Under the above sub-heading there appeared in our report of the. Warrington Recruiting Tribunal last week the following :- "The application of the foreman in the letter-press department of tho Co-operative Wholesale Society for postponement for a month or five weeks was refused." - _

The. heading was an unfortunate play upon words—that a letter-arrsx worker had been pressed; unfortunate because the word in the circumstances had another meaninc, than the one we intended.. As a matter of fact, the fore- man in question had attested readily, and it was his employers who applied for his exemp- tion. We very much regret that the heading caused him annoyance, and willingly give thi4 explanation with our ,apologies. •

PRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE COMPANY, LIMITED. Chief Office: HOLBORN BARS, LONDON. Summary of the Report presented at the Sixty-seventh Annual Meeting, held on March 2nd, 1916. : ! • • ORDINARY BRANCH.—The number of policies issued during the. year was 88,785, assuring the sum of £b,619.21b, and producing a new annual premium income of £457,217. The premiums received during the year were £5,157.619, being an increase of £121,881 over the year 1914. The claims of the year amounted to £4,338,768. of which £145,538 was in respect of War Claims. The number of deaths was 11,358. The number of endowment assurances matured was 25,559, the premium income of which was £137,797. The number of policies in force at the end of the year was 935.510. INDUSTRIAL BRANCH.—The premiums re- ceived during the year were £5,506,003, being an increase ot £329,861. t•• The claims of the year amounted to £3,938,598, of which £425,490 was in -respect of 25,379 War Claims. The bonus additions included in the claims amounted to £270,721. The total number of claims ,• fi and surrenders, including 15,505 endowment assur- ances matured. was 429,510. The number of free policies granted during the r...., , year to those policyholders of five years' standing .1 ; and upwards who desired to discontinue their pay- ments was 83,384, the number in force being 1,984,523. The number of free policies which became claims during the year was 61,417. 1 •-• P The total number of policies in force in this Branch. at the end of the year was 20,659,887; their average duration exceeds thirteen years. The War Claims paid during the year, in both Branches, number 28.826 and amount to £571,035. ,- • The total paid up to the present'on this account , - 1•': since the outbreak of War exceeds £750,900 in re- i • spect of over 34,509 claims. . 1 GENERAL BRANCH.—Under the Sickness In- surance Tables the premiums received during the year were mos, and £5,408 was paid in Sickness • claims. The whole of the Fund of £16,955 is re- served for future, liabilities. ..' The assets of the Company, in all branches, as .. shown in the balance sheet, are £94,794.798, being an increase of £3,592,454 over those of 1914. . • The Directors, after careful consideration, feel " . justified in paying a bonus on all participating ,e policies of the Ordinary Branch which become claims either by death or maturity during the finan- - • cial year: but in view of the present unsettled condi- tions it is not proposed to make a general distribu- tion of bonus, and the shareholders will not there- fore receive any part of the profits of this Branch. • The interests of participating policyholders are safe- , i guarded by a Special Contingency Fund cif i £700,000. t 'i The provisions relating to Industrial Assurance , contained in the Courts (Emergency Powers) Act, : 4!, 1914, have resulted in a severe strain upon the Cora- ,. pany's resources, which has reduced the surplus ; shown on the operations of the year, and whilst , . these provisions remain in force the strain must continue. In these circumstances the Directors , have not felt justified in drawing upon the £300,000 >, set aside last year to meet contingent liabilities created by the Act, but have met the loss out of revenue, and in addition have felt it necessary to .i. , increase the amount set aside by £60,000. The Courts (Emergency Powees) Act Reserve therefore stands, as at 31st December, 1915. at £350,000. The profit sharing scheme in the Industrial Branch provides that after payment of a fixed dividend to. the shareholders any surplus profit shall be divided into six parts; one part 'being retained by the shareholders, one distributed among the outdoor . staff of the Company, and the remaining four parts , being allotted by way of bonus to the policyholders . of the Industrial Branch., The sum which has already been paid or allotted under this scheme by way of bonus to the Industrial Branch policyholders ,it . and outdoor staff amounts to £2,825,000.
